h\in C(\mathcal{X})
and
\|D_{x}h\|_{op}\leq\|h\|L
(compare to
[
31
, Cor.4.36]
) then the condition is fulfilled.

In the following,
\beta_{d}
denotes
d
-dimensional Lebesgue measure of the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mu_{d}
denotes Lebesgue measure.

Proposition 2
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=[0,1]^{d}
and let
\mathcal{H}
be an RKHS such that for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
,

|h(x)-h(x^{\prime})|\leq L\|h\|\|x-x^{\prime}\|
. Furthermore, let
P
be a probability measure on
\mathcal{X}
and assume that
P
has a density
p
with
\inf_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\geq c>0
. Then for any
h\in\mathcal{H}
,
\left\lVert h\right\rVert\leq 1
,

\max_{y\in X}\left\langle-h,k(y,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\right\rangle\geq\frac{c\gamma^{d+1}}{(d+1)(2L)^{d}}\beta_{d}.

whenever there exists an
x\in\mathcal{X}
such that
\left\langle h,k(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\right\rangle\geq\gamma>0
and
\gamma/L\leq 1
.

Proof.

Fix any
h
in the unit ball of
\mathcal{H}
and let
f(x)=\left\langle h,k(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\right\rangle
. Let
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
be a point at which
f(x^{*})=\left\langle h,k(x^{*},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\right\rangle\geq\gamma
. The function
f
is also Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz-constant
\|h\|L\leq L
and
f
is therefore non-negative on the set
A=\{y:\|y-x^{*}\|\leq\gamma/L,y\in\mathcal{X}\}
.
Let
B=\{y:\|y\|\leq\gamma/L,y\in\mathcal{X}\}
then
P(A)\geq c\mu_{d}(A)\geq c\mu_{d}(B)
because
B
minimizes the volume of the intersection of
\mathcal{X}
with a ball of radius
\gamma/L
. Furthermore,
\mu_{d}(B)=(\gamma/2L)^{d}\beta_{d}
; this is the volume of a
d
-dimensional ball of radius
\gamma/L
scaled by
2^{-d}
. Now, integrating over
A
and using
[
18
, 265G, 265H]
again

\displaystyle\int_{A}f(x)\,dP(x)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{A-x^{*}}c(f(x^{*})-L\|x\|)\,dx\geq c\int_{B}f(x^{*})-L\|x\|\,dx

\displaystyle\geq\frac{c\gamma^{d+1}}{(2L)^{d}}\beta_{d}-cL2^{-d}\frac{d}{d+1}\frac{\gamma^{d+1}}{L^{d+1}}\beta_{d}=\frac{c\gamma^{d+1}}{(2L)^{d}}\beta_{d}\left(1-\frac{d}{d+1}\right).

Since
\int_{X}f(x)\,dP(x)=0
there must be a point
y\in\mathcal{X}
such that

f(y)\leq-\frac{c\gamma^{d+1}}{(2L)^{d}}\beta_{d}\left(\frac{1}{d+1}\right).

∎

Under the conditions of the proposition we can state a lower bound on the size of a ball included in
C
around
\mathfrak{m}
.
Let
h\in\mathcal{H}
,
\|h\|=1
, and assume w.l.o.g. that
s:=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\langle h,k(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle\geq-\inf_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\langle h,k(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle=:i
. Then
s\geq(1/2)\text{{width}\,}_{h}(C)
and

\displaystyle i\geq\frac{cs^{d+1}}{(d+1)(2L)^{d}}\beta_{d}\geq\frac{c((1/2)\text{{width}\,}_{h}(C))^{d+1}}{(d+1)(2L)^{d}}\beta_{d}.

(14)

If we have a lower bound
b
on
\text{{width}\,}_{h}(C)
for all such
h
then we can conclude that there exists a ball of radius

\delta=\min\{(b/2),\frac{c(b/2)^{d+1}\beta_{d}}{(d+1)(2L)^{d}}\}

around
\mathfrak{m}
in
C
.

2.4.2
Assumptions on the covariance operator

Let us start with a useful relationship between
\bm{1}
and
\mathfrak{m}
whenever
\bm{1}
lies in the RKHS. For any measure
P
, with corresponding element
\mathfrak{m}
we have that
\langle\bm{1},\mathfrak{m}\rangle=1
. Also, for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
\langle k(x,\cdot),\bm{1}\rangle=1
and
C
lies within the closed affine subspace
\{h\in\mathcal{H}:\langle h,\bm{1}\rangle=1\}
, where closure follows from
h\mapsto\langle h,\bm{1}\rangle
being a continuous function and
\{1\}
being closed. Also, since
1=\langle\mathfrak{m},\bm{1}\rangle\leq\|\bm{1}\|\|\mathfrak{m}\|
it has to hold that
\|\mathfrak{m}\|\geq 1/\|\bm{1}\|
. An upper bound on
\|\bm{1}\|
is therefore giving us a lower bound on
\|\mathfrak{m}\|
. For instance, we can get a lower bound on
\|\mathfrak{m}\|
by inspecting the kernel function
k
of the RKHS in the sense that

\|\mathfrak{m}\|\geq 1/\inf\{c:c^{2}k\succeq\bm{1}\otimes\bm{1}\}.

(15)

One might be tempted to move to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
whenever
\bm{1}
does not lie in the RKHS
\mathcal{H}
; recall that
\mathcal{H}^{+}
has the kernel
k+\bm{1}\otimes\bm{1}
. Since
\mathcal{H}
can be regarded as a subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
we have that
\|\mathfrak{m}\|=\|\mathfrak{m}\|_{+}
; however, only for
h\in\mathcal{H}
do we have that
\int h\,dP=\langle\mathfrak{m},h\rangle_{+}
since
\bm{1}\in\mathcal{H}^{\perp}
by construction. But there is then an element
\mathfrak{m}^{+}\in\mathcal{H}_{+}
for which
P_{\mathcal{H}}\mathfrak{m}^{+}=\mathfrak{m}
and
(I-P_{\mathcal{H}})\mathfrak{m}^{+}=\langle\bm{1},\mathfrak{m}^{+}\rangle=1
, where
P_{\mathcal{H}}
is the orthogonal projection onto the subspace
\mathcal{H}
and
I
the identity operator. Now,
1=\langle\bm{1},\mathfrak{m}^{+}\rangle_{+}\leq\|\mathfrak{m}^{+}\|_{+}=1+\|\mathfrak{m}\|
and we only learn from this the trivial fact that
0\leq\|\mathfrak{m}\|
.

An alternative approach gives us more insight. Whenever there is a function
\tilde{\bm{1}}\in\mathcal{H}
such that
\|\bm{1}-\tilde{\bm{1}}\|_{\infty}\leq\beta<1
then
1-\beta\leq\langle\mathfrak{m},\tilde{\bm{1}}\rangle\leq 1+\beta
and

\|\mathfrak{m}\|\geq(1-\beta)/\|\tilde{\bm{1}}\|=(1-\beta)/\inf\{c:c^{2}k\succeq\tilde{\bm{1}}\otimes\tilde{\bm{1}}\}.

(16)

In the following we will make use of the covariance operator
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}
(see Section
1.9
) to determine the location of
\mathfrak{m}
. Before exploring the relation between the covariance operator and the location of
\mathfrak{m}
we note the following adaptation of the above discussion: If
\bm{1}\in\mathcal{H}
then

\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}\bm{1},\bm{1}\rangle=1
and whenever
\tilde{\bm{1}}\in\mathcal{H}
fulfills
\|\bm{1}-\tilde{\bm{1}}\|_{\infty}\leq\beta<1
then

\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}\bm{1},\bm{1}\rangle\geq(1-\beta)^{2}\|\tilde{\bm{1}}\|=(1-\beta)^{2}/\inf\{c:c^{2}k\succeq\tilde{\bm{1}}\otimes\tilde{\bm{1}}\}.

(17)

Coming now to the problem of locating
\mathfrak{m}
within
C
we can take note of the following fundamental relationship.
Whenever
\mathcal{H}
is
d
-dimensional,
\|k\|_{\infty}<\infty
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}
has an eigen-decomposition with smallest eigenvalue
\bar{\lambda}_{d}
then for any
h\in\mathcal{H}
,
\|h\|=1
, with
\langle h,\mathfrak{m}\rangle=0
, it follows that
\int h(x)\,dP=0
and

\int(\langle h,k(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle)^{2}\,dP=\int h^{2}(x)\,dP\geq\bar{\lambda}_{d}.

Since
\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|h(x)|\leq\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}
and
\int h(x)\,dP=0
, a short calculation shows that

r(\mathfrak{m},h)\geq\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{d}}{\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}},

where with
h\in\mathcal{H}
,

r(\mathfrak{m},h):=(\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\langle h,k(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle)\wedge(-\inf_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\langle h,k(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle).

In detail, it is sufficient to consider the case of a discrete measure where with probability
p
the function
\langle h,k(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle
attains value
a
and with probability
1-p
attains value
b
, with
a<0<b
, and
a^{2}p+b^{2}(1-p)=\bar{\lambda}_{d}
. The condition
\langle h,\mathfrak{m}\rangle=0
then implies that
ap+b(1-p)=0
. For a particular value of
a
we get that
p=b/(b-a)
and
b=\bar{\lambda}_{d}/(-a)
. The value
b
is minimized by maximizing
-a
but
-a=\langle h,k(x,\cdot)\rangle
for some
x\in\mathcal{X}
and

-a=|a|\leq\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}
. By symmetry we get that
(-a)\wedge b\geq\bar{\lambda}_{d}/\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}
.

The remaining direction we have to take care of is
h^{*}=\mathfrak{m}/\|\mathfrak{m}\|

The distance of
\mathfrak{m}
to the boundary in direction
h^{*}
can be lower bounded away from zero when the smallest eigenvalue of the covariance operator is sufficiently large since

\int(\langle\mathfrak{m},k(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle)^{2}\,dP=\int(\langle\mathfrak{m},k(x,\cdot)\rangle)^{2}\,dP-\|\mathfrak{m}\|^{4}=\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}\rangle-\|\mathfrak{m}\|^{4}

and

\int(\langle h^{*},k(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle)^{2}\,dP=\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}h^{*},h^{*}\rangle-\|\mathfrak{m}\|^{2}\geq\bar{\lambda}_{d}-\|\mathfrak{m}\|^{2}.

Also,
|\langle h^{*},k(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle|\leq\|k(x,\cdot)\|+\|\mathfrak{m}\|\leq 2\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}
and

r(\mathfrak{m},h^{*})\geq\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{d}-\|\mathfrak{m}\|^{2}}{2\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}}.

For this approach to yield a useful bound
\bar{\lambda}_{d}
has to be strictly greater than
\|\mathfrak{m}\|^{2}
.
However,
\bar{\lambda}_{d}
can even be smaller than
\|\mathfrak{m}\|^{2}
. A better bound can be gained by using an eigen-decomposition of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}=\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}-\mathfrak{m}\widehat{\otimes}\mathfrak{m}
. In the following let
\bar{\lambda}_{1},\bar{\lambda}_{2},\ldots
be the eigenvalues of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}
then by the same argument as for
\mathfrak{C}
, whenever
h\in\mathcal{H}
,
\|h\|=1
, is such that
\langle h,\mathfrak{m}\rangle=0
, it follows that
\int(\langle h,k(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle)^{2}dP=\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}h,h\rangle\geq\bar{\lambda}_{d}
, where
\bar{\lambda}_{d}
is the smallest eigenvalue of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}
, and
r(\mathfrak{m},h)\geq\bar{\lambda}_{d}/\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}
.
For
h^{*}=\mathfrak{m}/\|\mathfrak{m}\|
we get now that

\int(\langle h^{*},k(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle)^{2}\,dP=\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}h^{*},h^{*}\rangle\geq\bar{\lambda}_{d}

and

r(\mathfrak{m},h^{*})\geq\bar{\lambda}_{d}/2\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}
. Also notice that when
\bm{1}\in\mathcal{H}
then
\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}\bm{1},\bm{1}\rangle=0
and
\bar{\lambda}_{d}=0
.
We summarize these finding in the following proposition.

Proposition 1
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A},P)
be some probability space with measurable kernel function
k
defined on it and such that the corresponding RKHS
\mathcal{H}
has dimension
d<\infty
. Furthermore, assume that
\|k\|_{\infty}<\infty
and that the centered covariance operator
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}
has an eigen-decomposition with smallest eigenvalue
\bar{\lambda}_{d}>0
. Then
\bm{1}\not\in\mathcal{H}
and for any
h\in\mathcal{H},\|h\|=1
,

r(\mathfrak{m},h)\geq\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{d}}{2\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}}.

The
dimension dependence
is in this result not as obvious as in Proposition
2
, but observe that when
\mathcal{X}
is the
d
-dimensional unit sphere,
d=2l+1
for some
l\in\mathbb{N}
,
P
is the Lebesgue measure restricted to
\mathcal{X}
and normalized,
k(x,y)=\langle x,y\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
for any
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{H}
is the corresponding RKHS which is of dimension
d
, then any
h\in\mathcal{H},\|h\|=1,

is of the form
\langle x,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
for some
x\in\mathcal{X},\|x\|_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}=1
and for such an
h

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}h,h\rangle

\displaystyle=\beta_{d}^{-1}\int h^{2}(y)\,dP(y)=\beta_{d}^{-1}\int\langle x,y\rangle^{2}\,dP(y)

\displaystyle=\beta_{d}^{-1}\int_{-1}^{1}\langle x,\tilde{y}x\rangle^{2}\mu_{d-1}(B_{d-1}(\sqrt{1-\tilde{y}^{2}}))\,d\mu(\tilde{y})

\displaystyle=\beta_{d-1}\beta_{d}^{-1}\int_{-1}^{1}\tilde{y}^{2}(1-\tilde{y}^{2})^{l}\,d\mu(\tilde{y})=\frac{2^{l}l!\beta_{d-1}\beta_{d}^{-1}}{\prod_{i=1}^{l}(2i+1)}\int_{-1}^{1}\tilde{y}^{2(l+1)}\,d\mu(\tilde{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{l}l!\beta_{d-1}\beta_{d}^{-1}}{(\prod_{i=1}^{l}(2i+1))(l+3/2)}=\frac{(2l+1)!}{l!2^{l+1}(\prod_{i=1}^{l}(2i+1))(l+3/2)}=\frac{1}{d+2},

where
\mu_{d-1}
denotes here the
d-1
-dimensional Lebesgue measure and
\beta_{d-1}
the Lebesgue measure of the
d-1
-dimensional unit sphere. Hence, the eigenvalues of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}
shrink to zero as the dimension
d
increases.

If
\bm{1}\in\mathcal{H}
then we get a similar result with
\bar{\lambda}_{d}
being replaced by
\bar{\lambda}_{d-1}
.

Corollary 1
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A},P)
be some probability space with measurable kernel function
k
defined on it and such that the corresponding RKHS
\mathcal{H}
has dimension
d<\infty
and
\bm{1}\in\mathcal{H}
. Furthermore, assume that
\|k\|_{\infty}<\infty
and that the centered covariance operator
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}
has an eigen-decomposition with eigenvalue
\bar{\lambda}_{d-1}>0
. Then for any
h\in\mathcal{H},\|h\|=1,\langle h,\bm{1}\rangle=0
,

r(\mathfrak{m},h)\geq\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{d-1}}{2\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}}.

Proof.

First note that
\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}\bm{1},\bm{1}\rangle=0
and for any
h\in\mathcal{H}
s.t.
\langle h,\bm{1}\rangle=0
,
\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}\bm{1},h\rangle=\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}h,\bm{1}\rangle=E(h)-E(h)=0
. In particular,
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}\bm{1}=0
and for any
h\in\mathcal{H},\|h\|=1,\langle h,\bm{1}\rangle=0
,

\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}h,h\rangle\geq\bar{\lambda}_{d-1}
. Now, for
h\in\mathcal{H},\|h\|=1,\langle h,\bm{1}\rangle=0
,

\displaystyle\int(\langle h,k(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle)^{2}\,dP=\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}h,h\rangle\geq\bar{\lambda}_{d-1}

and the lower bound follows by the same argument as in Proposition
1
.

∎

2.4.3
Data attaining values in a subset

Unless
h\in\mathcal{H}
is constant on the support of
P
it holds that
E((h-E(h))^{2})>0
and by the above arguments it follows that
r(\mathfrak{m},h)>0
.
But
\mathfrak{m}
can certainly lie in the boundary, for example, when
\mathfrak{m}=k(x,\cdot)
and
k(x,\cdot)
is an extreme of
C
. Therefore, there must be some direction
h
in which
r(\mathfrak{m},h)=0
. The point is that when
\mathfrak{m}
is an extreme or lies in a face of the convex set
C
and this face has extremes
\{k(x,\cdot):x\in S\subsetneq\mathcal{X}\}
then
P(X\in\mathcal{X}\setminus S)=0
has to hold. Furthermore, there then exists a normal
h^{*}
to this face and
h^{*}
is constant on the support of
P
which implies that
\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}h^{*},h^{*}\rangle=0
.

This observation suggests that
\mathfrak{m}
will either be an extreme or there will be a ball in an affine subset of
\mathcal{H}
around
\mathfrak{m}
within a face of the convex set. Furthermore, we can hope that this affine subspace is directly related to the non-zero eigenvalues of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}
and that these eigenvalues characterize a lower bound on the width of this ball. Alternatively, it is natural to consider the space
\mathcal{H}_{S}=\{h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S:h\in\mathcal{H}\}
where
S
is the support of
P

[
18
, Def.411N]
. The space
\mathcal{H}_{S}
is again an RKHS with kernel
k_{S}=k\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\times S
and norm
\|h\|_{k_{S}}=\inf\{\|u\|:u\!\upharpoonright\!S=h,u\in\mathcal{H}\}

[
26
, Cor.5.8]
. In the proposition below we show that the covariance operator
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}^{S}
corresponding to
P
and
\mathcal{H}_{S}
characterizes the ball around
\mathfrak{m}
within the affine subspace spanned by
C
.

Proposition 3
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A},P)
be a probability space with
P
being a topological
\tau
-additive probability measure which has support
S
and let
k
be
a continuous kernel function
k
defined on
\mathcal{X}
such that the corresponding RKHS
\mathcal{H}
has dimension
d<\infty
. Furthermore, assume that
\|k\|_{\infty}<\infty
, and that the centered covariance operator
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}
has an eigen-decomposition with eigenvalue such that (i)

\bar{\lambda}_{l}>0=\bar{\lambda}_{l+1}
for some
l<d
or (ii)
\bar{\lambda}_{d}>0
. It follows that
\mathcal{H}_{S}
has dimension
l+1
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{S}_{c}
has eigenvalues

\bar{\lambda}^{S}_{i}=\bar{\lambda}_{i}
for all
i\leq l
and
\bar{\lambda}_{l+1}^{S}=0
under (i), and
\mathcal{H}_{S}
has dimension
d
and the eigenvalues of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{S}_{c}
are the same eigenvalues as of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}
under (ii). Furthermore, under condition (ii) there exists a closed ball
B
centered at
\mathfrak{m}
with radius
\bar{\lambda}_{d}/2\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}
inside
C
. If
\lambda_{1}=0
and there are no two points
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
such that
k(x,x)=k(y,y)=k(x,y)
then
S
consists of a single element and
P=k(x,\cdot)
for some
x\in\mathcal{X}
. If
\lambda_{1}>0
then
\mathfrak{m}
lies in the relative interior of
F=\text{{cch}\,}\{k(x,\cdot):x\in S\}
. In particular, under condition (i) there exists a closed ball
B
centered at
\mathfrak{m}
such that
B\cap\text{{aff}\,}F\subset F
and
B
has radius

\bar{\lambda}_{l}/2\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}
.

Proof.

(a)
When
\bar{\lambda}_{d}>0
it follows that
E(h^{2}(X))>E(h(X))^{2}
for all
h\in\mathcal{H},h\not=0
. In particular, let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{d}\in\mathcal{H}
be linearly independent and fix any
a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}
are not simultaneously equal to zero. Consider
e_{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S,\ldots,e_{d}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S
then

E\bigl(\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{d}a_{i}e_{i}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\bigr)^{2}\bigr)=E\bigl(\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{d}a_{i}e_{i}\bigr)^{2}\bigr)>0.

Since this holds for all such
a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}
it follows that
e_{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S,\ldots,e_{d}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S
are linearly independent and
\mathcal{H}_{S}
is
d
-dimensional. Similarly, when
\lambda_{l}>0
and
\lambda_{l+1}=0
it follows that there is an
h_{\bm{1}}\in\mathcal{H}_{S}
which is almost surely equal to
\bm{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S
and
l
linearly independent functions
e_{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S,\ldots,e_{l}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\in\mathcal{H}
which are also linearly independent of
h_{\bm{1}}
(for any non-trivial linear combination of
e_{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S,\ldots,e_{l}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S
it follows that the second moment is strictly larger than the squared expected value and therefore these linear combinations are not equal to a constant function). In fact,
h_{\bm{1}}
is equal to
\bm{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S
since by assumption the kernel function is continuous: Assume that
h_{\bm{1}}
is not equal to
\bm{1}
, take a
x\in S
such that
h_{\bm{1}}(x)\not=1
and let
\epsilon=|h_{\bm{1}}(x)-1|
. Take a function
h\in\mathcal{H}
such that
h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S=h_{\bm{1}}
and
\|h\|\leq\|h_{\bm{1}}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}+\epsilon/4
. The set
A=h^{-1}[\{y:|y-h_{\bm{1}}(x)|<\epsilon/4\}]
is open and has non-empty intersection with
S
. Also,
h_{\bm{1}}
is different from
\bm{1}
on all of
A
. Due to
[
18
, 411N]
it follows that
P(A)>0
and
\bm{1}
is not almost surely equal to
h_{\bm{1}}
which is impossible. By the same argument it follows that the other eigenfunctions of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}
with zero eigenvalues are constant on
S
and therefore lie in the span of
h_{\bm{1}}
and the dimension of
\mathcal{H}_{S}
is
l+1
.

(b)
In case (ii), let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{d}
be orthonormal in
\mathcal{H}
then, due to (a), the functions
e_{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S,\ldots,e_{d}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S
are linearly independent. Also
\|e_{i}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\|_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}=\|e_{i}\|
for all
i\leq d
, because for any given
i\leq d
,
e_{i}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S
does not lie in the linear subspace spanned by
\{e_{j}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\}_{j\not=i}
. Similarly, for any
i,j\leq d
,
\|e_{i}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S+e_{j}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\|_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}=\|e_{i}+e_{j}\|
and
\|e_{i}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S+e_{j}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}=\|e_{i}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\|_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}^{2}+\|e_{j}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}
. Hence,

\{e_{i}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\}_{i\leq d}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{S}
. In particular, when
e_{1},\ldots,e_{d}
are the eigenfunctions of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}
then

e_{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S,\ldots,e_{d}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S
are the eigenfunctions of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{S}_{c}
since for
i\not=j
,

\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{S}_{c}e_{i}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S,e_{j}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}=\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}e_{i},e_{j}\rangle=0

and for any
i\leq d
,
\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{S}_{c}e_{i},e_{i}\rangle=\bar{\lambda}_{i}
.

By the same argument it follows that in case (i) that
e_{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S,\ldots,e_{l}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S,\bm{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S/\|\bm{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\|_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{S}
. Hence, if
e_{1},\ldots,e_{l}
are the first
l
eigenfunctions of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}
then
e_{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S,\ldots,e_{l}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S
are the eigenfunctions of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{S}_{c}
and the eigenvalues match.

(c)
When
\bar{\lambda}_{1}=0
it follows that
\mathcal{H}_{S}=\text{{span}\,}\{\bm{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\}
and
\langle\mathfrak{m}_{S},c\bm{1}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}=c=\langle\mathfrak{m}_{S,n},c\bm{1}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}
for all
c\in\mathbb{R}
where
\mathfrak{m}_{S}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{S,n}
are just
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{n}
when the kernel is restricted to
S
. Also, every function
h\in\mathcal{H}
is constant on
S
and
\langle\mathfrak{m},h\rangle=\langle\mathfrak{m}_{n},h\rangle=\langle h,k(x,\cdot)\rangle
for all
x\in S
. In particular, if
S
does not consist of a single element it follows that
k(x,y)=k(x,x)=k(y,y)
for all
x,y\in S
. Reversing this statement leads to the claim made in the proposition.

(d)
Let
U=\text{{aff}\,}F
be the affine subspace spanned by
k(x,\cdot),x\in S
. The element
\mathfrak{m}
lies within
U
since for any
h\in\mathcal{H}
for which
\langle k(x,\cdot),h\rangle=c
for all
x\in S
and for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
it follows that
\langle\mathfrak{m},h\rangle=\int_{S}\langle k(x,\cdot),h\rangle\,dP=c
. In other words, if
\mathfrak{m}
would not lie in
U
then there would be an
h,\|h\|>0,
that stands perpendicular on
U
and such that
\mathfrak{m}=h+\argmin_{g\in U}\|g-\mathfrak{m}\|
, and
\langle\mathfrak{m},h\rangle\not=\langle k(x,\cdot),h\rangle
for all
x\in S
.

(e)
Under condition (ii) the constant functions are not in
\mathcal{H}
and the conditions of Proposition
1
are fulfilled and the existence of the ball with the specified radius follows directly.

Under condition (i) the constant functions are contained in
\mathcal{H}_{S}
and there exists a function
h_{0}\in\mathcal{H}
such that
h_{0}(x)=1
for all
x\in S
. Consider
\inf\{\|h\|:h(x)=1\text{ for all }x\in S\}\leq\|h_{0}\|
. Since
h\mapsto\|h\|
is continuous and
\{h:\|h\|\leq\|h_{0}\|\}
is compact it follows that the infimum is attained at some
h^{*}\in\mathcal{H}
. Also,
\|\bm{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\|_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}=\inf\{\|h\|:h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S=\bm{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\}=\|h^{*}\|
and
\bm{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S=h^{*}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S
.

Let
U=\text{{aff}\,}\{k(x,\cdot):x\in S\}\subset\mathcal{H}
and
V=U-U
be the subspace parallel to
U
. For any
h\in U
there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\lambda_{1}+\ldots+\lambda_{m}=1
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\in S
such that
h=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}k(x_{i},\cdot)
. In particular, if
g\in\mathcal{H}
is constant on
S
and attains value
c
, then
\langle g,h\rangle=c\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}=c
. This implies that
g
is orthogonal to
V
since
\langle g,h_{1}-h_{2}\rangle=0
for all
h_{1},h_{2}\in U
.
Due to assumption (i) there are
d-l
eigenfunctions
e_{l+1},\ldots,e_{d}
of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}
which are constant on
S
, that is
e_{l+1},\ldots,e_{d}
are orthonormal and each of them is orthogonal to
V
. Also, any function
h
that is orthogonal to
V
has to be constant on
S
since
h(x)=\langle k(x,\cdot),h\rangle=\langle k(y,\cdot),h\rangle=h(y)
for all
x,y\in S
. Since the eigenfunctions
e_{1},\ldots,e_{l}
have corresponding eigenvalues which are strictly greater than zero it follows that
e_{1},\ldots,e_{l}
cannot be constant and
V
has dimension
l
. Also, note that
U
cannot be equal to
V
, or better,
U
cannot be a subspace but only an affine subspace: assume otherwise then
0\in U
and
if
h
is constant on
S
, attaining value
c\not=0
, then
0=\langle h,k(x,\cdot)\rangle=h(x)=c
for all
x\in S
. In other words there cannot be functions that are constants on
S
in
\mathcal{H}
, but we know already that there are functions which are constant on
S
in
\mathcal{H}
.

(f)
We claim that
W=\text{{span}\,}(V\cup\{h^{*}\})
, when equipped with the inner product of
\mathcal{H}
, is isometric isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}_{S}
,
U\subset W
and
(\text{{span}\,}\{h^{*}\})\cap U\not=\emptyset
. We start with the latter claim. Since
U
is not a subspace it follows that the orthogonal projection of
0
onto
U
is not
0
itself. In detail, take any
x\in S
and let
P_{V}
the projection onto the subspace
V
then
the orthogonal projection onto
U
is the operator defined by
P_{U}h=k(x,\cdot)+P_{V}(h-k(x,\cdot))
. Now
P_{U}0=k(x,\cdot)-P_{V}k(x,\cdot)\not=0
and,
P_{U}0
is orthogonal to
V
. But that means the
P_{U}0
is constant on
S
and lies in the span of
e_{l+1},\ldots,e_{d}
. In particular, there is function that is constant on
S
which lies in
U
.
Notice that for any function
h
which is constant on
S
there exists an element in
U
that lies in the span of
h
if
\|h\|=\sup_{g\in U}|\langle h,g\rangle|/\|g\|
.
Recall that a function
g\in U
can be written as
g=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}k(x_{i},\cdot)
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\in S
and such that
\lambda_{1}+\ldots+\lambda_{d}=1
. Hence,
\langle h,g\rangle=c
if
h
attains value
c
and
h\in U
if
\|h\|=|c|\sup_{g\in U}1/\|g\|
. Consider now a function
h
that is constant on
S
and attains value
1
then
\|h\|=\sup_{g\in U}1/\|g\|
. For
0<\epsilon<\|h^{*}\|
take
g\in U
such that
\|h\|\leq\epsilon+1/\|g\|
, then

\frac{|\langle h^{*},g\rangle|}{\|h^{*}\|}\leq\|g\|\leq\frac{|\langle h,g\rangle|}{\|h\|-\epsilon}=\frac{|\langle h^{*},g\rangle|}{\|h\|-\epsilon}\leq\frac{|\langle h^{*},g\rangle|}{\|h^{*}\|-\epsilon}.

In other words,

\frac{|\langle h^{*},g\rangle|}{\|g\|}\leq\|h^{*}\|\leq\frac{|\langle h^{*},g\rangle|}{\|g\|}+\epsilon

and
\sup_{g\in U}|\langle g,h^{*}\rangle|/\|h^{*}\|=\|h^{*}\|
which implies that
(\text{{span}\,}\{h^{*}\})\cap U\not=\emptyset
and
U\subset W

Let
\psi:W\to\mathcal{H}_{S}
be the function that associates with
h\in W
the function
h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\in\mathcal{H}_{S}
. The function
\psi
is linear since
(af+g)\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S=a(f\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S)+g\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S
for all
f,g\in W
,
a\in\mathbb{R}
. We have seen already that
\psi(h^{*})=\bm{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S
and
\|\psi(h^{*})\|_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}=\|h^{*}\|
.
Also any
h\in U
lies in the span of
\{k(x,\cdot):x\in S\}
and, since
\mathcal{H}
is finite dimensional,
h
can be written as
\sum_{i=1}^{d}a_{i}k(x_{i},\cdot)
for some
a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}\in\mathbb{R}
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}\in S
. Hence, any
g,h\in U\subset W
can be written as
h=\sum_{i=1}^{d}a_{i}k(x_{i},\cdot)
,
g=\sum_{i=1}^{d}b_{i}k(y_{i},\cdot)
, with some
a_{i},b_{i}\in\mathbb{R},x_{i},y_{i}\in S
for all
i\leq d
, and

\langle g,h\rangle=\sum_{i,j}^{d}a_{i}b_{j}k(x_{i},y_{j})=\sum_{i,j}^{d}a_{i}b_{j}k_{S}(x_{i},y_{j})=\langle\psi(g),\psi(h)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}.

But this implies that for any
g,h\in V
, that is in the subspace parallel to
U
, there exists
\tilde{g},\tilde{h}\in U
and
x\in S
such that

g=\tilde{g}-k(x,\cdot),h=\tilde{h}-k(x,\cdot)
and

\displaystyle\langle g,h\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\tilde{g}-k(x,\cdot),\tilde{h}-k(x,\cdot)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\psi(\tilde{g}),\psi(\tilde{h})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}-\langle\psi(\tilde{g}),k_{S}(x,\cdot)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}-\langle\psi(\tilde{h}),k_{S}(x,\cdot)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}+k_{S}(x,x)

\displaystyle=\langle\psi(\tilde{g}-k(x,\cdot)),\psi(\tilde{h}-k(x,\cdot))\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}

\displaystyle=\langle\psi(g),\psi(h)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{S}},

since
\psi(k(x,\cdot))=k_{S}(x,\cdot)
for any
x\in S
. Finally, for any
g\in V
, write
g=\tilde{g}-k(x,\cdot)
for some
x\in S
,
\tilde{g}\in U
then

\langle g,h^{*}\rangle=\langle\tilde{g},h^{*}\rangle-\langle k(x,\cdot),h^{*}\rangle=0=\langle\psi(\tilde{g})-k_{S}(x,\cdot),\bm{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}=\langle\psi(g),\psi(h^{*})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{S}},

since
h^{*}
is constant on
S
,
\psi(\tilde{g})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\psi(k(x_{i},\cdot))
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\in S
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}=1
, and
\langle\psi(\tilde{g}),\bm{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}=1
. Hence,
\psi
is an isometry and since
\mathcal{H}_{S}
and
\text{{span}\,}(V\cup\{h^{*}\})
have the same it follows that they are isometric isomorphic. The existence of the ball around
\mathfrak{m}
of the specified radius follows now directly from Corollary
1
.
∎

Notice that
\mathcal{H}_{S}
does not have to be equal to
\mathcal{H}
when
\bar{\lambda}_{d}>0
. For instance, when
\mathcal{H}
consists of the quadratic functions on
[-1,1]
and has therefore dimension
1
. If the measure
P
is discrete with
P(\{-1\})=P(\{1\})=1/2
then
\bar{\lambda}_{1}>0
but
S=\{-1,1\}
and
\mathcal{H}_{S}\not=\mathcal{H}
.

2.5
Locating
\mathfrak{m}_{n}
within the empirical convex set

We are now combining the various results we have derived. Section
2.4
allows us to refer the size of a ball within
C
around
\mathfrak{m}
back to the question of the width of
C
. In Section
2.3
we derived various ways to lower bound the width of
C
. We also know that
C_{n}
converges to
C
. Section
2.2
contains various results on that. These results combine Rademacher or VC bounds with lower bounds on
P\psi_{\gamma}(\langle u,\phi(\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle-c
and
Pf_{u,c}
. These lower bounds are closely related to the bounds in Section
2.4
since in both settings we need to measure how much probability mass lies in various directions behind some threshold. To get now high probability bounds for the existence of ball of a certain size around
\mathfrak{m}_{n}
within
C_{n}
we also need to control the convergence of
\mathfrak{m}_{n}
to
\mathfrak{m}
. But that is easy to do with another VC or Rademacher argument. The following two theorems combine these results under some natural conditions. The first result applies when
\mathcal{X}=[0,1]^{l}
,
\mathcal{H}
is finite dimensional, that functions in
\mathcal{H}
are Lipschitz continuous and that we have lower bound on the density of the law of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
on
\mathcal{X}
. We also assume that
\bm{1}\in\mathcal{H}
but the result can easily be adapted to the case of
\bm{1}\not\in\mathcal{H}
.

Theorem 1
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=[0,1]^{l},l\geq 1,
and
k
a continuous kernel function on
\mathcal{X}
such that the corresponding RKHS
\mathcal{H}
is
d
-dimensional,
1\leq d<\infty
, functions
h\in\mathcal{H}
are Lipschitz continuous in the sense of (
13
) with Lipschitz constant
L>0
, and
\bm{1}\in\mathcal{H}
. Furthermore, let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be i.i.d. random variables defined on some probability space and such that the law
P
of
X_{1}
has a density
p
on
\mathcal{X}
and
\inf_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\geq c>0
for some constant
c
. Mercer’s theorem applies to
k
. Let
\tilde{\lambda}_{d}
be the smallest eigenvalue of the Mercer decomposition. There exists a ball of radius

\delta=2\tilde{\lambda}_{d}^{1/2}\wedge\frac{2c\tilde{\lambda}_{d}^{(l+1)/2}\beta_{l}}{(l+1)L^{l}}

around
\mathfrak{m}
in
C
in the affine subspace spanned by
C
. Furthermore, for any
q\in(0,1)
and whenever

n>\left(\frac{\sqrt{2\log(6/q)}+96\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}/\delta}{c\beta_{l}(\delta/8L)^{l}}\right)^{2}\vee\left(\frac{4\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}+3\sqrt{2\log(3/q)}}{\delta/4}\right)^{2}

then with probability
1-q
there exists a ball of radius
\delta/4
around
\mathfrak{m}_{n}
in
C_{n}
within the affine subspace spanned by
C
.

Proof.

The existence of the ball around
\mathfrak{m}
in
C
has already been derived at the end of Section
2.4.1

and the bound on the width in terms of the lowest eigenvalue of the Mercer decomposition has been stated in Proposition
1
.

(a)
We start with high probability bounds for
\|\mathfrak{m}_{n}-\mathfrak{m}\|
being small using Rademacher complexities. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
be a countable dense subset of the unit ball of
\mathcal{H}_{-}
then for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,

\Pr\left(\|\mathfrak{m}_{n}-\mathfrak{m}\|\geq\alpha\right)=\Pr\left(\sup_{h\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}|(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{i})-Pf|\geq\alpha\right)

since,
\|\mathfrak{m}_{n}-\mathfrak{m}\|=\sup_{h\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}\langle h,\mathfrak{m}_{n}-\mathfrak{m}\rangle
. In particular, for any
q>0
and
\alpha=4n^{-1/2}\|k\|_{\infty}+3\sqrt{2\log(1/q)}n^{-1/2}

we can infer from
[
5
, Lem.22]
and
[
19
, Thm.3.4.5]
that,

\Pr\left(\|\mathfrak{m}_{n}-\mathfrak{m}\|\geq(4\|k\|^{1/2}_{\infty}+3\sqrt{2\log(1/q)})n^{-1/2}\right)\leq q.

(18)

(b)
Next, we expand the argument from Section
2.2.3
to control the difference between
C_{n}
and
C
. Let
c^{\prime}=-\delta/2
then
for any
\gamma>0
and with probability
1-q
simultaneously for all
u\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
,

\displaystyle P_{n}\psi_{\gamma}(\langle u,\phi(\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle-c^{\prime})

(19)

\displaystyle\geq P\psi_{\gamma}(\langle u,\phi(\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle-c^{\prime})-(\sqrt{2\log(2/q)}+24\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}/\gamma)n^{-1/2}.

Chose
\gamma=\delta/4
and let
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
be a point such that
\langle u,\phi(x_{0})-\mathfrak{m}\rangle\leq-\delta
.
Then,

\displaystyle P\psi_{\gamma}(\langle u,\phi(\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle-c^{\prime})\geq\Pr(\langle u,\phi(X_{1})-\mathfrak{m}\rangle\leq-\gamma+c^{\prime}).

As in the proof of Proposition
2
let
A=\{y:\|y-x_{0}\|\leq\delta/4L,y\in\mathcal{X}\}
and
B
the translation of
A
to the origin,
B=\{y:\|y\|\leq\delta/4L\}
. Then the Lebesgue measure of
B
is
\mu_{l}(B)=(\delta/8L)^{l}\beta_{l}
and
\Pr(X_{1}\in A)\geq c\mu_{l}(B)
. Hence,

\Pr(\langle u,\phi(X_{1})-\mathfrak{m}\rangle\leq-\gamma+c^{\prime})\geq\Pr(X_{1}\in B)\geq c\beta_{l}(\delta/8L)^{l}.

For a given
q
let

N_{q}=\left(\frac{\sqrt{2\log(2/q)}+96\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}/\delta}{c\beta_{l}(\delta/8L)^{l}}\right)^{2}

then whenever
n>N_{q}
with probability
1-q
there is a ball of radius
\delta/2
around
\mathfrak{m}
in
C_{n}
.

(c)
Finally, we transfer the lower bound that we have for a ball within
C_{n}
around
\mathfrak{m}
to
\mathfrak{m}_{n}
. For
q\in(0,1)
let

\tilde{N}_{q}=\left(\frac{4\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}+3\sqrt{2\log(1/q)}}{\delta/4}\right)^{2}.

Then for any
n>\tilde{N}_{q}
with probability at least
1-q
,
\|\mathfrak{m}_{n}-\mathfrak{m}\|\leq\delta/4
.

Bringing this together, with probability
1-q
there is ball of size
\delta/4
in
C_{n}
around
\mathfrak{m}_{n}
whenever
n>N_{q/3}\vee\tilde{N}_{q/3}
.

∎

The second result uses an assumption on the centered covariance operator instead of an assumption on the density. For this result we actually do not need to use the results on the minimal width of
C
. We forumlate this result directly for the case where
P
is allowed to attain values in a strict subset of
\mathcal{X}
. A fortunate circumstance in that setting is that the empirical convex set converges to the intersection of
C
with the minimal face that contains
\mathfrak{m}
and algorithms that work with the empirical convex set adapt automatically to the structure of the covariance operator. The result relies on the existence of the support of the measure
P
. A weak assumption to guarantee this existence is that
P
is a
\tau
-additive topological measure
[
18
, 411N]
.

Theorem 2
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A},P)
be some probability space with
P
being a topological measure that is
\tau
-additive, and
with measurable kernel function
k
defined on
\mathcal{X}
such that the corresponding RKHS
\mathcal{H}
is finite dimensional. Furthermore, let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be i.i.d. random variables attaining values in
\mathcal{X}
and with law
P
and assume that
\|k\|_{\infty}<\infty
, and that the centered covariance operator
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}
has an eigen-decomposition with smallest non-zero eigenvalue being
\bar{\lambda}_{d}
. There exists a ball of radius
\delta=\bar{\lambda}_{d}/2\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}
around
\mathfrak{m}
in
C
within the affine subspace spanned by
C
.
Furthermore, for any
q\in(0,1)
and whenever
n
is (strictly) greater than

\left(\frac{8\|k\|_{\infty}(\sqrt{2\log(6/q)}+192\|k\|_{\infty}/\bar{\lambda}_{d})}{\bar{\lambda}_{d}^{2}}\right)^{2}\vee\left(\frac{16\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}+\sqrt{288\log(2/q)}}{\delta}\right)^{2}

then with probability
1-q
there exists a ball of radius
\delta/4
around
\mathfrak{m}_{n}
in
C_{n}
within the affine subspace spanned by
C
.

Proof.

(a)
The existence of the ball with radius
\delta
follows directly from Proposition
3
. Furthermore, the same high probability bound for
\|\mathfrak{m}_{n}-\mathfrak{m}\|
as in the proof of Theorem
1
applies. The bound for
C_{n}
also runs along the same line as in Theorem
1
. Consider in the following the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{S}
. Let
\gamma=\delta/4
and
c=-\delta/2
then Equation (
5
) tells us that
P\psi_{\gamma}(\langle u,\phi_{S}(\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{S}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{S}}-c)\geq\bar{\lambda}^{2}/8\|k\|_{\infty}
whenever
u\in\mathcal{H}_{S}
has unit norm. Hence, with probability
1-q
and simultaneously for all
u\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
, where
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
is countable dense subset of the unit ball of
\mathcal{H}_{S}
,

P_{n}\psi_{\gamma}(\langle u,\phi(\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}\rangle-c^{\prime})\\
\geq\bar{\lambda}_{d}^{2}/8\|k\|_{\infty}-(\sqrt{2\log(2/q)}+192\|k\|_{\infty}/\bar{\lambda}_{d})n^{-1/2}.

(b)
For a given
q\in(0,1)
let

\tilde{N}_{q}=((16\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}+12\sqrt{2\log(1/q)})/\delta)^{2}
then for any
n>\tilde{N}_{q}
,
\|\mathfrak{m}_{n}-\mathfrak{m}\|\leq\delta/4
with probability
1-q
. Similarly, with probability
1-q
for
n>N_{q}
there is a ball of radius
\delta/2
around
\mathfrak{m}
in
C_{n}
(as a subset of the affine subspace spanned by
C
), where

N_{q}=\frac{64\|k\|^{2}_{\infty}(\sqrt{2\log(2/q)}+192\|k\|_{\infty}/\bar{\lambda}_{d})^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}_{d}^{4}}.

∎

The convergence of the empirical mean embedding and empirical convex set are both unproblematic in the large sample case in both theorems. The bottleneck of the approach is rather the size of the convex set
C
itself.

3
Related approximation problems

When confronted with a concrete statistical problem it is typically insufficient to only approximate
\mathfrak{m}
. For example, the least-squares error, when a regressor
f
from an RKHS
\mathcal{H}
with kernel
k
is used, is

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(f(X_{i})-Y_{i})^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle f\otimes f,k(X_{i},\cdot)\otimes k(X_{i},\cdot)\rangle_{\otimes}-\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle f,Y_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot)\rangle+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}^{2}.

Looking at the right hand side we can note that we have to deal with
multiple approximation problems
. There are two high level approaches to addressing multiple approximation problems. We can either solve each approximation problem individually or we can solve them simultaneously. In terms of finding core-sets this means that we will get three different core-sets when solving the approximation problems individually and a single core-set when we solve the approximation problems simultaneously. Before getting back to this discussion, let us have a look at the individual terms in the above least-squares problem.

The
third term
on the right hand side is rather unproblematic since it does not depend on
f
and can be summarized by a single real number. In particular, if we approximate each term individually then we can compress this term down to a single real number. The

first term
on the right hand side
corresponds to an empirical covariance and can be treated in a similar way to the empirical measure, i.e.

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}k(X_{i},\cdot)\otimes k(X_{i},\cdot)

attains values in
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
. It fact, since we are only interested in the terms
f^{2}(X_{i})
,
there is an RKHS that is better suited for our purposes than
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
. Due to
[
26
, Thm.5.16]
there exists a function
g\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
such that

f^{2}(X_{i})=g(X_{i})
, where
\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
is the RKHS that corresponds to the kernel function
\kappa(x,y)=k^{2}(x,y)
. The empirical covariance, when restricted to
\{(h,h):h\in\mathcal{H}\}
, can be identified with

\mathfrak{C}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa(X_{i},\cdot)\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H},

i.e. for any
h\in\mathcal{H}
,

\langle\mathfrak{C}_{n},h^{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h^{2}(X_{i})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle k(X_{i},\cdot)\otimes k(X_{i},\cdot),h\otimes h\rangle_{\otimes}.

The random element
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
attains values within the empirical convex set

C_{\odot,n}:=\text{{cch}\,}\{\kappa(X_{i},\cdot):i\leq n\}.

The corresponding population covariance element is given by

\mathfrak{C}=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\kappa(x,\cdot)\,dP(x)

(20)

which is contained in the convex set

C_{\odot}=\text{{cch}\,}\{\kappa(x,\cdot):x\in\mathcal{X}\}.

In Section
2.4.2

we used the covariance operator
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
. Notice that
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}
and
\mathfrak{C}
are closely related since for any
h\in\mathcal{H}
,

\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}h,h\rangle=E(h^{2}(X))=\langle\mathfrak{C},h^{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}
.

The
second term
in the above sum is more difficult to deal with than the other two due to the elements
Y_{i}
.
We are looking at two approaches in Section
3.3
: In the first approach we consider
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}=(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot)
as a subset of
\text{{cch}\,}\{Y_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot):i\leq n\}
. That approach works well when we consider the approximation problem in isolation, but it does lead to complications when trying to approximate
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
simultaneously to
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
. In the second approach we incorporate the
Y_{i}
’s into the kernel by using
\langle Y_{i},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes k(X_{i},\cdot)
as a kernel function and by mapping
f\in\mathcal{H}
to
\langle 1,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes f(\cdot)\in{\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
, i.e. for
i\leq n
,

\langle\langle 1,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes f(\cdot),\langle Y_{i},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes k(X_{i},\cdot)\rangle_{{\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}}=Y_{i}f(X_{i})=\langle f,Y_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot)\rangle.

In this approach we are aiming to approximate

\mathfrak{m}^{\otimes}_{y,n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle Y_{i},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes k(X_{i},\cdot)=\langle 1,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}.

In the least-squares problem we might like to use the same points
X_{i}
with the same weights
w_{i}
to

approximate
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
simultaneously
. As we mentioned above this approach is facilitated by incorporating the
Y_{i}
’s and by moving to
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}
. Similarly, it is useful to extend the functions in
\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{X}
by setting
\hat{h}(y,x)=h(x)
for
h\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
. We denote the resulting space by
\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}
which is again a Hilbert space when using the inner product
\langle\hat{h},\hat{g}\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}}=\langle h,g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}
, for any
g,h\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
.
In fact, it is an RKHS with kernel function
\kappa_{y}((y_{1},x_{1}),(y_{2},x_{2}))=\kappa(x_{1},x_{2})
, since

\langle\hat{h},\kappa_{y}((y,x),\cdot)\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}}=\langle h,\kappa(x,\cdot\rangle)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}=h(x)=\hat{h}(y,x).

The empirical covariance operator now becomes
\mathfrak{C}_{y,n}=(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{y}((Y_{i},X_{i}),\cdot)
.

One way to achieve a simultaneous approximation of
\mathfrak{C}_{y,n}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}
is to use a direct sum
\mathcal{G}=(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})\oplus({\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H})
and consider the convex set

C_{\oplus,n}=\text{{cch}\,}\{(\kappa((Y_{i},X_{i}),\cdot),\langle Y_{i},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes k(X_{i},\cdot)):i\leq n\}\subset\mathcal{G}.

The element that we like to approximate is in this context
(\mathfrak{C}_{y,n},\mathfrak{m}^{\otimes}_{y,n})
which lies in
C_{\oplus,n}
. Let
(\bar{\mathfrak{C}}_{y,n},\bar{\mathfrak{m}}_{y,n}^{\otimes})
be some element in
\mathcal{G}
, then

\displaystyle\|(\bar{\mathfrak{C}}_{y,n},\bar{\mathfrak{m}}_{y,n}^{\otimes})-(\mathfrak{C}_{y,n},\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes})\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}=\|\bar{\mathfrak{C}}_{y,n}-\mathfrak{C}_{y,n}\|_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}}^{2}+\|\bar{\mathfrak{m}}^{\otimes}_{y,n}-\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}\|_{{\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}}^{2}

and a good approximation in
\mathcal{G}
guarantees good approximations of
\mathfrak{C}_{y,n}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}
simultaneously.

3.1
Assumptions

There are some
minimal assumptions
that we need to impose on
\mathfrak{C},\mathfrak{m}_{y}
and variations thereof to be well defined.
Generally, we assume that we have independent pairs of random variables
(X,Y),(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots
defined on some probability space

(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mu)
. For
\mathfrak{C}
to be well-defined it suffices to assume that
\kappa(Y,\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mu;\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})
and, similarly, for
\mathfrak{m}_{y}
it suffices to assume that
Y\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mu)
and
k(X,\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mu;\mathcal{H})
since
then
\int\|Yk(X,\cdot)\|\,d\mu\leq\|Y\|_{2}\bm{\|}k(X,\cdot)\bm{\|}_{2}<\infty
and

\mathfrak{m}_{y}=\int Yk(X,\cdot)\,d\mu\in\mathcal{H}
.

Some
further assumptions
are useful to facilitate the following analyses. In particular, in the least-squares setting it is natural to assume that

Y=f_{0}(X)+\epsilon
, where
f_{0}
is a suitable function,
\epsilon
is a zero mean real-valued random variable representing measurement noise, and
X
and
\epsilon
are independent. When making this assumption we are assuming that
\epsilon
is a random variable that is defined on the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mu)
. To guarantee that
Y\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mu)
it is enough to assume that
f_{0}(X)\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mu)
and
\epsilon\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mu)
.

3.2
Covariance operators

Since Equation (
20
) tells us that the covariance operator
\mathfrak{C}
can be treated like a mean element after changing the kernel, it follows immediately that the techniques we developed for approximating
\mathfrak{m}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{m}
can be applied to the covariance operator
\mathfrak{C}
and its empirical version
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
.
One might also wonder if the approximation problems for
\mathfrak{m}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
are related and if any information that we might deduce about
C_{n}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{n}
can give us insights into the approximation problem for
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
. For instance, can we say anyhting about the width of the convex set in
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}
based on the width of the convex set in
\mathcal{H}
? Or, does an assumption on the variance of functions in
\mathcal{H}
translate to statements about the variance of certain functions in
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}
? Unsurprisingly, this seems to be impossible to do in general. However, for certain functions in
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}
we can infer statements about the width and the variance. Similarly, under very stringent assumptions on certain eigenvalues we can say something about the width of the convex set with respect to any function in
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}
. Before looking into these questions we start by taking a closer look at the RKHS
\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
.

Note that the space
\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
is not just the RKHS corresponding to the kernel
\kappa(x,y)=k^{2}(x,y)
but it is also closely related to the tensor product
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
. In particular,
h\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
if, and only if, there exists
u\in\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
such that
h(x)=u(x,x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, and then
\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}=\inf\{\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}}:h(x)=u(x,x)\text{ for all }x\in\mathcal{X}\}

[
26
, Thm.5.16]
. Observe that for any

h\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
the set
A_{h}:=\{u\in\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}:h(x)=u(x,x)\text{ for all }x\in\mathcal{X}\}
is a convex subset of
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
. Define the linear operator
T:\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
by
Tu=u\circ\psi
where
\psi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X},\psi(x)=(x,x)
, and observe that
T
is bounded since
\|Tu\|_{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}\leq\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}}
. Hence,
A_{h}=T^{-1}[\{h\}]
is closed. Also, observe that when

h=(f\otimes g)\circ\psi
for some
f,g\in\mathcal{H}
then there exists functions
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\in\mathcal{H}
such that
h=(1/2)(f_{1}\otimes f_{1}-f_{2}\otimes f_{2}-f_{3}\otimes f_{3})\circ\psi
and
\|f\otimes g\|_{\otimes}\geq\|f_{1}\otimes f_{1}+f_{2}\otimes f_{2}+f_{3}\otimes f_{3}\|_{\otimes}
: choose
f_{1}=(f-g),f_{2}=f,f_{3}=g
then

f\otimes g+g\otimes f=(f+g)\otimes(f+g)-(f\otimes f)-(g\otimes g)=f_{1}\otimes f_{1}-f_{2}\otimes f_{2}-f_{3}\otimes f_{3}

and, since
(f\otimes g)\circ\psi=(g\otimes f)\circ\psi
, the first statement follows. In terms of the norm observe that
\|f\otimes g+g\otimes f\|_{\otimes}=\|f_{1}\otimes f_{1}-f_{2}\otimes f_{2}-f_{3}\otimes f_{3}\|_{\otimes}\leq 2\|f\|\|g\|=2\|f\otimes g\|_{\otimes}
. Also, note that

(1/2)\|f\otimes g+g\otimes f\|_{\otimes}^{2}=\|f\|^{2}\|g\|^{2}+|\langle f,g\rangle|^{2}=\|f\otimes g\|_{\otimes}^{2}+|\langle f,g\rangle|^{2}(\leq 2\|f\otimes g\|^{2}),

where the expression in the bracket follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Hence, when

f
and
g
are orthogonal, we have that

\|f_{1}\otimes f_{1}-f_{2}\otimes f_{2}-f_{3}\otimes f_{3}\|_{\otimes}=\sqrt{2}\|f\otimes g\|_{\otimes}.

The point is that for any tensor
f\otimes g
we can express
(f\otimes g)\circ\psi
as a linear combination of ‘symmetric’ tensor elements applied to
\psi
without increasing the tensor norm.

Let us next consider the closed subspace
U=\overline{\text{{span}}}\,\{k(x,\cdot)\otimes k(x,\cdot):x\in\mathcal{X}\}\subset\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
and the orthogonal projection
P_{U}
onto it. For any
u\in\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
we have that
P_{U}u
lies in the subspace spanned by
k(x,\cdot)\otimes k(x,\cdot)
and
\|P_{U}u\|_{\otimes}\leq\|u\|_{\otimes}
. In fact, for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle u\circ\psi(x)

\displaystyle=\langle u,k(x,\cdot)\otimes k(x,\cdot)\rangle_{\otimes}=\langle u,P_{U}(k(x,\cdot)\otimes k(x,\cdot))\rangle_{\otimes}

\displaystyle=\langle P_{U}u,k(x,\cdot)\otimes k(x,\cdot)\rangle_{\otimes}=(P_{U}u)\circ\psi(x).

In other words, for
h\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
, if we can show that the infimum will be attained over
A_{h}
, then there exists an element
u
in
U
such that
h=u\circ\psi
,
\|u\|_{\otimes}=\|h\|_{\odot}
, and for any
v\not\in U
that fulfills

v\circ\psi=h
it follows that
\|v\|_{\otimes}>\|u\|_{\otimes}
.

When
\mathcal{H}
is finite dimensional and the kernel function is bounded it follows that
A_{h}\cap U
is compact: the tensor space
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
is finite dimensional since
\mathcal{H}
is finite dimensional. Also,
h
is bounded since the kernel function is bounded. For
\epsilon>0
consider the centered closed ball
B
of radius
\|h\|_{\odot}+\epsilon
within
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
. The intersection
B\cap A_{h}\cap U
is non-empty and compact and the infimum is attained within this compact set.

3.2.1
Lower bound on the width of
C_{\odot}

How can this
tensor product characterization
of
\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
be used to characterize the width of
C_{\odot}
? Let us first consider tensors of the form
f\otimes f
,
f\in\mathcal{H}
,
\|f\otimes f\|=1=\|f\|
. The width of
C_{\odot}
in direction
h
, where
h=(f\otimes f)\circ\psi
, is

\displaystyle\text{{width}\,}_{h}C_{\odot}

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}h(x)-\infd_{x\in\mathcal{X}}h(x)

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\langle f\otimes f,k(x,\cdot)\otimes k(x,\cdot)\rangle_{\otimes}-\infd_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\langle f\otimes f,k(x,\cdot)\otimes k(x,\cdot)\rangle_{\otimes}

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f^{2}(x)-\infd_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f^{2}(x).

If
\mathcal{X}
is
path connected
,
k
is
continuous
and, hence,
f
is continuous, then we can relate
\text{{width}\,}_{h}C_{\odot}
to

\text{{width}\,}_{f}(C)
: whenever there exist
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
such that
f(x)>0>f(x^{\prime})
, then there also exists an
\tilde{x}\in\mathcal{X}
with
f(\tilde{x})=0
due to the mean value theorem. In this case,

\text{{width}\,}_{h}C_{\odot}\geq(\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)\vee-\infd_{x\in X}f(x))^{2}\geq((1/2)\text{{width}\,}_{f}(C))^{2}.

If there is no
x
such that
f(x)=0
, that is
f
attains only positive or only negative values, then we can argue in the following way. W.l.o.g. assume that
f
attains only positive values. Since for
a,b\geq 0
,
a\geq b
,
a^{2}-b^{2}\geq(a-b)^{2}
,
it also follows in this case that

\text{{width}\,}_{h}C_{\odot}\geq((1/2)\text{{width}\,}_{f}(C))^{2}.

This lower bound can fail to hold when the assumptions about
k
and
\mathcal{X}
are not fulfilled
. Consider
\mathcal{X}=\{-1,1\}
with
k(x,y)=\delta_{x,y}
and the function
f=k(1,\cdot)-k(-1,\cdot)
which lies in the RKHS. For this function
f
,

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f^{2}(x)-\infd_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f^{2}(x)=0<1=((1/2)(\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)-\infd_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)))^{2}.

The factor
1/2
in the lower bound is redundant when
f
attains only positive or negative values since

a^{2}-b^{2}\geq(a-b)^{2}
whenever
a
and
b
have the same sign. More importantly, the bound becomes loose when
\infd_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f^{2}(x)
is large since
a^{2}-b^{2}-(a-b)^{2}=2b(a-b)
whenever
a\geq b>0
.

Moving on to other directions
h\in\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
,
\|h\|_{\otimes}=1
, we can first observe that the arguments are not as straightforward as elements in
U
are of the form

\sum_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}k(x_{i},\cdot)\otimes k(x_{i},\cdot)
for some
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and
x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}
for
i\leq d
, and, when considering the supremum over
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the different terms
\alpha_{i}k^{2}(x_{i},x)
can potentially cancel each other; in particular, there is no reason why the
\alpha_{i}
should all be positive.

Alternatively, we can consider the set
S=\{k(x,\cdot)\otimes k(x,\cdot)-\int k(z,\cdot)\otimes k(z,\cdot)dP(z):x\in\mathcal{X}\}
and the convex hull of
S
. This convex hull is closely related to
C_{\odot}
. Note that
\text{{ch}\,}S
is a subset of
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
and contains the origin. Fix now any
u\in\text{{span}\,}S
,
\|u\|_{\otimes}=1
, then
\text{{span}\,}\{u\}
intersects
\text{{ch}\,}S
. In fact,
\text{{span}\,}\{u\}\cap\text{{ch}\,}S
consists of more than a single point since otherwise
\langle k(x,\cdot)\otimes k(x,\cdot),u\rangle_{\otimes}=\langle k(y,\cdot)\otimes k(y,\cdot),u\rangle_{\otimes}
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
and
\langle u,s\rangle_{\otimes}=0
for any
s\in S
which would imply that
u=0
. Consider the two points at which
\text{{span}\,}\{u\}
intersects with the boundary of
\text{{ch}\,}S
and let
d
be the dimension of
\text{{span}\,}S
. Each of these points can be expressed as a convex combination of
l\leq d+1
points in
S
due to Carathéodory’s theorem. Hence,
u=\tilde{u}/\|\tilde{u}\|
where
\tilde{u}=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}k(x_{i},\cdot)\otimes k(x_{i},\cdot)
for some strictly positive
\alpha_{i}
’s that sum to one and suitable points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}
. We can note right away that

\inf_{x\in\mathcal{X}}k(x,x)/(d+1)\leq\Bigl(\sum_{i,j=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}k^{2}(x_{i},x_{j})\Bigr)^{1/2}=\|\tilde{u}\|_{\otimes}\leq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}k(x,x).

(21)

Also, when
k(x,y)
is a non-negative function
an application of Jensen’s inequality yields further results. In detail,

\displaystyle\sup_{x,y\in\mathcal{X}}\tilde{u}(x,x)-\tilde{u}(y,y)=\sup_{x,y\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}(k^{2}(x_{i},x)-k^{2}(x_{i},y))

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Bigl(\,\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}k(x_{i},x)\Bigr)^{2}-\infd_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}k^{2}(x_{i},y).

In the following, let
c=\inf_{g}\text{{width}\,}_{g}(C)
, where the infimum is taken over
\{g:g\in\mathcal{H},\|g\|=1\}
. When
c
is large,
k
is non-negative and
\|k\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, then this simple argument might be of use: since
k(x_{i},y)\leq 1
it follows that
k(x_{i},y)\geq k^{2}(x_{i},y)
and

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Bigl(\,\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}k(x_{i},x)\Bigr)^{2}-\infd_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}k^{2}(x_{i},y)\geq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Bigl(\,\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}k(x_{i},x)\Bigr)^{2}-\infd_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}k(x_{i},y).

Furthermore,
(\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}k(x_{i},y))^{2}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}k(x_{i},y)-1/4
and

\sup_{x,y\in\mathcal{X}}\tilde{u}(x,x)-\tilde{u}(y,y)\geq c-1/4.

This is only useful for large
c
. If, in fact,
c>1/4
, we can proceed and

\sup_{x,y\in\mathcal{X}}u(x,x)-u(y,y)\geq\frac{c-1/4}{\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}k(x,x)}\geq c-1/4.

3.2.2
Lower bounds on the fourth moments

Instead of controlling the width of
C_{\odot}

we can also aim to control the covariance operator corresponding to the kernel
\kappa
. Effectively, this corresponds to bounds on the fourth moments. A bound on the non-centered fourth moment is, in fact, easy to derive: let
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}
be the covariance operator (now interpreted as a linear operator) corresponding to the kernel
k
. We need to control
E((h(X)-E(h(X))^{2})
for a function
h\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}
. Choosing again a suitable
u\in\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
such that
h=u\circ\psi
, we find that

\displaystyle E(h^{2}(X))=E((u\circ\psi(X))^{2}).

If
U
is
d
-dimensional then, as above, we can write
u=\tilde{u}/\|\tilde{u}\|
where
\tilde{u}=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}k(x_{i},\cdot)\otimes k(x_{i},\cdot)
for some positive
\alpha_{i}
,
\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i}=1
, and suitable points
x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}
with
l\leq d+1
. Given this representation of
u
and
assuming that the smallest eigenvalue of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}
is
\bar{\lambda}
,

\displaystyle\|\tilde{u}\|^{2}E(h^{2}(X))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}E((k(x_{i},X)k(x_{j},X))^{2})\geq\sum_{i,j=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}|\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}k(x_{i},X),k(x_{j},X)\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}^{2}\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{k(x_{i},x_{i})}.

In particular,
when
k(x,x)=1
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
then

E(h^{2}(X))\geq\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{(d+1)\|\tilde{u}\|^{2}}\geq\frac{\bar{\lambda}}{d+1}

follows from Eq. (
21
).

However, to say something about the largest ball that lies around
\mathfrak{C}
within
C_{\odot}
we need a lower bound on the variance of
h\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}
. This is not straight-forward and will need, in all likelihood, some stringent assumptions: consider the variance of an arbitrary functions
h\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}
, when
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}
has dimension
d<\infty
. By the above argument, there exists a
u=\tilde{u}/\|\tilde{u}\|
, where
\tilde{u}=\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i}k(x_{i},\cdot)\otimes k(x_{i},\cdot)
for some non-negative
\alpha_{i}
that sum to one and points
x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}
. Hence,

\displaystyle E((h(X)-E(h(X)))^{2})=E((u\circ\psi(X)-E(u\circ\psi(X)))^{2})

\displaystyle=\|\tilde{u}\|^{-2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}E((k^{2}(x_{i},X)-E(k^{2}(x_{i},X)))(k^{2}(x_{j},X)-E(k^{2}(x_{j},X)))).

There is no reason why the sum over the off-diagonal elements

\sum_{i\not=j}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}E((k^{2}(x_{i},X)-E(k^{2}(x_{i},X)))(k^{2}(x_{j},X)-E(k^{2}(x_{j},X))))

should be positive or should be of considerably smaller magnitude than the sum over the diagonal elements.

3.3
Weighted mean embedding

There are different ways to address the term
Y_{i}f(X_{i})
,
i\leq n
, that occurs in the least squares problem and there are a variety of natural assumptions under which one can study the corresponding compression problem. Let us first have a look at how
Y_{i}f(X_{i})
can be lifted into the RKHS so that we can apply the compression techniques. A first approach to do so was introduced at the beginning of Section
3
, where we wrote
Y_{i}f(X_{i})=\langle f,Y_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot)\rangle
. Using this representation we can try to approximate

(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot)\in\mathcal{H}
. A second approach is to map the
Y_{i}
’s to linear functionals, that is to elements in the dual space
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
, and to consider the tensor products

\langle Y_{i},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes k(X_{i},\cdot).

We still would like to work with an inner product similarly to
\langle f,k(X_{i},\cdot)\rangle
and we can do so if we work with
\langle 1,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes\langle f,\cdot\rangle
instead of
f
. In particular,

Y_{i}f(X_{i})=\langle\langle Y_{i},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes k(X_{i},\cdot),\langle 1,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes\langle f,\cdot\rangle\rangle_{\otimes}.

(22)

Both approaches are natural when the
Y_{i}
’s are bounded but various issues arise when they are not. In particular, for the latter approach the elements
\langle Y_{i},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes k(X_{i},\cdot)
are not contained with probability one in a ball in the corresponding tensor product space. We therefore discuss the case where the
Y_{i}
’s are bounded first before moving on to the unbounded case. Finally, it is often natural to impose an assumption on the relation between
X_{i}
and
Y_{i}
, like

Y_{i}=f_{0}(X_{i})+\epsilon_{i}

(23)

with
f_{0}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(P),\epsilon_{i}
independent of
X_{i}
,
E(\epsilon_{i})=0
, and the
X_{i}
and
\epsilon_{i}
are i.i.d.

This leaves us with a total of eigth different settings. But not all of these settings are useful for deepening our understanding. In particular, little can be said without the assumption Eq. (
23
) and we assume in the following, up to short discussions, that Eq. (
23
) holds. Beyond that we focus on three settings: the first setting uses the assumption that
Y_{i}
is bounded and we use the form
Y_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot)
. We then move on to translate the results to the approach where we model
Y_{i}f(X_{i})
through the tensor product as in Eq. (
22
). Finally, we lift the assumption that
Y_{i}
is bounded and study the problem in the context of the assumptions Eq. (
22
)
and Eq. (
23
). We assume throughout that the
X_{i}
’s are i.i.d. and that
k(X_{i},\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(P;\mathcal{H})
.

3.3.1
First setting: Bounded
Y_{i}
’s &
Y_{i}f(X_{i})=\langle f,Y_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot)\rangle

There are few natural question when working with the empirical estimate
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}=(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot)
: what is a natural convex set which contains
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
and over which we can optimize efficiently? Do we have suitable population limits of the empirical quantities? What can be said about the diameter of the empirical convex set, about how centered
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
lies within the set and are assumptions on the covariance operator of use? In terms of an empirical convex set which contains
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
it is natural to consider the set

C_{y,n}=\text{{cch}\,}\{Y_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot):i\leq n\}

and optimization over this set is possible since we have control over the extremes of it.

Under the assumption that the
Y_{i}
’s are of the form
f_{0}(X_{i})+\epsilon_{i}
, there are natural expressions for the population limits
\mathfrak{m}_{y}
and the convex set
C_{y}
. For concreteness, we assume in the following that
\mathcal{X}
is a
Borel space
,
\mathcal{H}
is
separable
,

f_{0}
and the feature map
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{H}
are measurable,
f_{0}
, the kernel function
k
and the
\epsilon_{i}
’s are
bounded
,
\epsilon_{i}
is independent of
X_{i}
, and
E(\epsilon_{i})=0
. We can define the population limit of
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
through

\mathfrak{m}_{y}=\int f_{0}(x)k(x,\cdot)\,dP(x),

where
P
is the law of
X_{1}
. The element
\mathfrak{m}_{y}
lies in
\mathcal{H}
: The function
f_{0}\times\phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{H}
is weakly measurable since when
h\in\mathcal{H}
, then
\langle f_{0}(x)\phi(x),h\rangle=f_{0}(x)h(x)
is the product of two Borel measurable functions and is therefore Borel measurable. Because
\mathcal{H}
is separable it follows that
f_{0}\times\phi
is Bochner measurable. Furthermore,
\|f_{0}(x)\phi(x)\|\leq|f_{0}(x)|k^{1/2}(x,x)
is a bounded function of
x
and
\mathfrak{m}_{y}=\int f_{0}(x)\phi(x)\,dP
is well defined and lies in
\mathcal{H}
.

Controlling
\|\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\|
.

We can quantify the deviation of
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
from
\mathfrak{m}_{y}
in the following way. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
be a countable dense subset of the unit ball of
\mathcal{H}
and using that dual elements can be moved through the Bochner integral, we get for
\alpha>0
,

\displaystyle\Pr(\|\mathfrak{m}_{y}-\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}\|\geq\alpha)

\displaystyle=\Pr\Bigl(\sup_{h\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}\langle h,\int f_{0}(X_{1})k(X_{1},\cdot)\,d\mu-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot)\rangle\geq\alpha\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\Bigl(\sup_{h\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}\int f_{0}(X_{1})h(X_{1})\,d\mu-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{0}(X_{i})h(X_{i})\geq\alpha/2\Bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\hskip 199.16928pt+\Pr\Bigl(\sup_{h\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}h(X_{i})\geq\alpha/2\Bigr).

(24)

The
latter term
can be bounded by means of Theorem 3.3 in
[
PIN94
]
which is a Bernsteintype theorem for Banach spaced valued random variables. Note that the theorem statement in
[
PIN94
]
contains and error which is corrected in
[
PIN99
]
. The bound is the following,

\sup_{h\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}h(X_{i})=\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\phi(X_{i})\|

and
v_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\phi(X_{i})
attains values in
\mathcal{H}
. The sequence
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
is a martingale sequence in
\mathcal{H}
with regard to the filtration
F_{t}=\sigma(X_{1},\ldots,X_{t},\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{t})
,
t\leq n
, since
E(v_{t}|F_{t-1})=E(\epsilon_{t}\phi(X_{t}))+v_{t-1}=v_{t-1}
(a.s.) for
2\leq t\leq n
due to the independence between
\epsilon_{t}
and
X_{t}
. Furthermore, let
v_{0}=0
and
F_{0}=\{\emptyset,\Omega\}
so that
E(v_{1}|F_{0})=E(v_{1})=v_{0}
a.s.
We can continue the sequence by letting
v_{t}=v_{n}
and
F_{t}=F_{n}
for all
t>n
, which preserves the martingale property. To apply
[
PIN94
,
PIN99
, Thm.3.3]
we need the following moment bounds; for all
m\geq 2
,

\sum_{t=1}^{n}E(\|\epsilon_{t}\phi(X_{i})\|^{m}|F_{t-1})\leq n(c\|k\|_{\infty})^{m}\text{\quad\quad(a.s.)},

where
c
is an upper bound on
|\epsilon_{t}|
. This implies that we can set
\Gamma=c\|k\|_{\infty},B=n^{1/2}c\|k\|_{\infty}
in
[
PIN94
,
PIN99
, Thm.3.3]
and

\Pr\big(\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\phi(X_{i})\|\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}\big)\leq 2\exp\Bigl(-\frac{n\alpha^{2}}{4c\|k\|_{\infty}(c\|k\|_{\infty}+\alpha+\sqrt{c^{2}\|k\|_{\infty}^{2}+\alpha c\|k\|_{\infty}/n})}\Bigr).

(25)

The
first term
can be controlled with a standard
Rademacher argument
after changing the kernel. Define the kernel
l=f_{0}\otimes f_{0}
and consider the product kernel
l\times k:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
with RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{l\times k}
. For
h\in\mathcal{H}
it follows that
f_{0}\times h\in\mathcal{H}_{l\times k}
; for an
h
of the form
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}k(x_{i},\cdot)
one can write down the representation explicitly as

f_{0}\times h=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}f_{0}(x)k(x_{i},x)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\alpha_{i}/f_{0}(x_{i}))(l\times k)(x_{i},x)
,
whenever
f_{0}(x_{i})\not=0
for all
i\leq m
.
We can thus write

\displaystyle\Pr\Bigl(\sup_{h\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}\int f_{0}(X_{1})h(X_{1})\,d\mu-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{0}(X_{i})h(X_{i})\geq\alpha/2\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\Pr\Bigl(\sup_{g\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{l\times k}}\int g(X_{1})\,d\mu-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(X_{i})\geq\alpha/2\Bigr),

(26)

where

\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{l\times k}
is dense subset of the unit ball of
\mathcal{H}_{l\times k}
.
The Rademacher argument that we are using in (
18
) can now be applied.

Population limit of
C_{y,n}
.

The next question to address is how to
define the population limit of
C_{y,n}
. Under the boundedness assumption of
Y_{1}
we can characterize the limit of
C_{y,n}
in the following way. Let

\bar{b}=\inf\{b:b\in\mathbb{R},\epsilon_{1}\leq b\text{ a.s.}\}
,
\underaccent{\bar}{b}=-\sup\{b:b\in\mathbb{R},\epsilon_{1}\geq b\text{ a.s.}\}
and let

C_{y}=\text{{cch}\,}(\{(f_{0}(x)+\bar{b})k(x,\cdot):x\in\mathcal{X}\}\cup\{(f_{0}(x)-\underaccent{\bar}{b})k(x,\cdot):x\in\mathcal{X}\}).

Note that for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
(f_{0}(x)+\bar{b})k(x,\cdot)
and
(f_{0}(x)-\underaccent{\bar}{b})k(x,\cdot)
lie in
\mathcal{H}
and
C_{y}\subset\mathcal{H}
. Furthermore,
\epsilon_{i}\in[-\underaccent{\bar}{b},\bar{b}]
(a.s.) and for
i\leq n
,
(f_{0}(X_{i})+\epsilon_{i})k(X_{i},\cdot)
is almost surely a convex combination of
(f_{0}(X_{i})+\bar{b})k(X_{i},\cdot)
and
(f_{0}(X_{i})-\underaccent{\bar}{b})k(X_{i},\cdot)
. Therefore,
C_{y,n}
is almost surely contained within
C_{y}
.

While
C_{y}
is a natural limit of
C_{y,n}
, we face the problem that the convergence towards
C_{y}
can be arbitrarily slow since
\epsilon_{1}
can have a low probability of attaining values close to
\bar{b}
or
\underaccent{\bar}{b}
. Assumptions on the distribution of
\epsilon_{1}
are one way to address this problem. Alternatively, we can work directly with
C_{y,n}
and study how deep
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
lies in
C_{y,n}
by controlling events of the form
\langle h,\tilde{Y}k(\tilde{X},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle\leq c
, where
(\tilde{X},\tilde{Y})
has law
P_{n}
, and by comparing the random variables
\langle h,\tilde{Y}k(\tilde{X},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle
and
\langle h,\tilde{Y}k(\tilde{X},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}\rangle
.
We follow this latter approach and we use Rademacher complexities to control these events uniformly over the unit ball of
\mathcal{H}
. In the Rademacher approach, we control such events by lower bounding terms of the form

P_{n}\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,\tilde{y}k(\tilde{x},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}\rangle-c)\quad\bigl(=\int\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,\tilde{y}k(\tilde{x},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}\rangle-c)\,dP_{n}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\bigr)

for suitable
c,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
and all unit norm elements
h\in\mathcal{H}
. The element
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
converges to
\mathfrak{m}_{y}
and, because
\psi_{\gamma}
is
1/\gamma
-Lipschitz continuous, it follows that

\displaystyle|P_{n}\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,\tilde{y}k(\tilde{x},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}\rangle-c)-P_{n}\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,\tilde{y}k(\tilde{x},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c)|

\displaystyle\leq P_{n}(1/\gamma)|\langle h,\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle|\leq(1/\gamma)\|\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\|,\text{\quad(a.s.)}

(27)

where the
P_{n}
term becomes redundant since no variables
\tilde{y}
and
\tilde{x}
are present in the last line.

Next, we consider the convergence of

P_{n}\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,\tilde{y}k(\tilde{x},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c)
to its population limit
P(\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,yk(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c))
uniformly over the unit ball of
\mathcal{H}
. The convergence can be controlled by using Rademacher complexities. Because
\epsilon
is used in this section to denote the noise terms we will use
\zeta
to denote Rademacher variables.
Since
\psi_{\gamma}
is continuous and is applied to a subset of
\mathbb{R}
we can note that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}\psi_{\gamma}(Y_{i}h(X_{i})-E(Yh(X))-c)

is well defined. Also,
\gamma\psi_{\gamma}
is a contraction vanishing at zero
[
19
, Sec.5.2.1]
and for any finite subset
F
of the unit ball of
\mathcal{H}
it follow that

\displaystyle E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{h\in F}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}(\gamma/2)\psi_{\gamma}(Y_{i}h(X_{i})-E(Yh(X))-c)\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{h\in F}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}Y_{i}h(X_{i})\bigr)\leq E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{h\in F}\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}Y_{i}h(X_{i})\bigr|\bigr)\text{\quad(a.s.)},

from
[
19
, Thm.5.2.1, Eq.5.50]
. Note that, conditional on
Y_{i}
, the probability laws of
\zeta_{i}Y_{i}
and
\zeta_{i}|Y_{i}|
are the same. In particular,

E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{h\in F}\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}Y_{i}h(X_{i})\bigr|\bigr)=E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{h\in F}\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}|Y_{i}|h(X_{i})\bigr|\bigr)\text{\quad(a.s.)}.

Recall that Rademacher complexities are stable under taking absolute convex hulls. Let
C_{F}=\{(|Y_{1}|h(X_{1}),\ldots,|Y_{n}|h(X_{n})):h\in F\}
and
\hat{C}_{F}=\{(\|Y\|_{\infty}h(X_{1}),\ldots,\|Y\|_{\infty}h(X_{n})):h\in F\}
then
\text{abs conv }C_{F}\subset\text{ abs conv }\hat{C}_{F}
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{h\in F}\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}|Y_{i}|h(X_{i})\bigr|\bigr)=E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{t\in C_{F}}\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}t_{i}\bigr|\bigr)=E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{t\in\text{abs conv }C_{F}}\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}t_{i}\bigr|\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{t\in\text{abs conv }\hat{C}_{F}}\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}t_{i}\bigr|\bigr)=\|Y\|_{\infty}E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{h\in F}\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}h(X_{i})\bigr|\bigr).\text{\quad(a.s.)}

In summary, we have shown that

E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{h\in F}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}\psi_{\gamma}(Y_{i}h(X_{i})-E(Yh(X))-c)\bigr)\leq(2/\gamma)\|Y\|_{\infty}E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{h\in F}\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}h(X_{i})\bigr|\bigr).\text{\quad(a.s.)}

A simple variation of the above argument gives us a bound on the absolute value. In detail,

\displaystyle E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{h\in F}\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}(\gamma/2)\psi_{\gamma}(Y_{i}h(X_{i})-E(Yh(X))-c)\bigr|\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{h\in F}\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}Y_{i}h(X_{i})\bigr|\bigr)+\sup_{h\in F}|E(Yh(X))-c|E\bigl(\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}\bigr|\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\|Y\|_{\infty}E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{h\in F}\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}h(X_{i})\bigr|\bigr)+(\|Y\|_{\infty}\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}+|c|)\sqrt{2\pi n},\text{\quad(a.s.)}

(28)

where the last inequality follows from integrating a Hoeffding bound on
\Pr(|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}|\geq t)
. Since this holds for all finite
F
we can take the supremum over finite sets
F
on both sides and move to
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
(see (
1
)).

Lower bounds.

With the Rademacher argument we control the difference between the empirical and population value. To make use of this bound we need a lower bound on the population value. This can be attained in the following way.
Let
p=\Pr(\epsilon_{1}\geq 0)\wedge\Pr(\epsilon_{1}\leq 0)
. Because
E(\epsilon_{1})=0
it holds that
p>0
. Using the towering rule for conditional expectations and that
\psi_{\gamma}
is monotonically decreasing, we can now argue in the following way for the population limit and any
h\in\mathcal{H}
,

\displaystyle E(\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,Yk(X,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c))

\displaystyle\geq E(\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,Yk(X,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c)\times\chi\{\epsilon h(X)\leq 0\})

\displaystyle\geq E(\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,f_{0}(X)k(X,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c)\times

\displaystyle\quad\quad(E(\chi\{\epsilon\leq 0\}|X)\times\chi\{h(X)\geq 0\}+E(\chi\{\epsilon\geq 0\}|X)\times\chi\{h(X)\leq 0\}))

\displaystyle\geq pE(\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,f_{0}(X)k(X,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c)).

(29)

The final expectation term can be dealt with in the usual way after moving to the kernel function
l\times k
, where
l=f_{0}\otimes f_{0}
. We demonstrate this for the case that we work with a covariance operator assumption and we derive high probability lower bounds on the radius of a ball centered on
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
which lies within
C_{y,n}
.

Assumptions on the covariance operator.

We denote the centered covariance operator for the kernel
l\times k
by
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c,l\times k}
. As usual, we need an assumption on the smallest non-zero eigenvalue of this operator. Recall that
\|f_{0}\|_{l}=1
and

\|f_{0}\times h\|_{l\times k}=\min\{\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{l}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{k}}:f_{0}(x)h(x)=u(x,x)\text{ for all }x\in\mathcal{X}\}.

In particular,
\|f_{0}\times h\|_{l\times k}\leq\|f_{0}\otimes h\|_{\mathcal{H}_{l}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\|h\|_{k}
. In fact, this can be tightened by using
[
26
, Prop5.20]
: the RKHS

\mathcal{H}_{l\times k}
is the set
\{f_{0}\times h:h\in\mathcal{H}\}
and for any
g,h\in\mathcal{H}
we have that
\langle f_{0}\times g,f_{0}\times h\rangle_{l\times k}=\langle g,h\rangle
.
In particular, all eigenfunctions of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c,l\times k}
are of the form
f_{0}\times h
for some
h\in\mathcal{H},\|h\|=1
. Therefore, our bounds will depend on

\bar{\lambda}_{\star}=\inf\{\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c,l\times k}f_{0}\times h,f_{0}\times h\rangle_{l\times k}:\|h\|=1,f_{0}\times h\in(\text{ker }\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c,l\times k})^{\perp}\}.

(30)

As before, it is beneficial to move to the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{l\times k,S}
corresponding to the kernel function
\kappa=(l\times k)\!\!\upharpoonright\!S\times S
, where
S
is the support of
X
. Observe that

(l\times k)\!\!\upharpoonright\!S\times S=(l\!\!\upharpoonright\!S\times S)\times(k\!\!\upharpoonright\!S\times S)=((f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)\otimes(f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S))\times(k\!\!\upharpoonright\!S\times S).

Using
[
26
, Prop5.20]
again shows that
\mathcal{H}_{l\times k,S}=\{(f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)\times h:h\in\mathcal{H}_{S}\}
, where
\mathcal{H}_{S}
is defined as before. Furthermore,
for
g,h\in\mathcal{H}_{S}
,

\langle(f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)\times g,(f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)\times h\rangle_{l\times k,S}=\langle g,h\rangle_{S}.

Let us also introduce
\mathfrak{m}_{l\times k,S}=\int\kappa(x,\cdot)\,dP(x)
, where
P
is the law of
X
, and which is well defined whenever
(l\times k)(X,\cdot)
is Bochner integrable. For
h\in\mathcal{H}
,

\displaystyle\langle h,\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\langle h,f_{0}(x)k(x,\cdot)\rangle\,dP(x)=\int_{S}(f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)(x)(h\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)(x)\,dP(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{S}\langle h\!\!\upharpoonright\!S,(f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)(x)(k\!\!\upharpoonright\!S\times S)(x,\cdot)\rangle_{S}\,dP(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{S}\langle(f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)\times(h\!\!\upharpoonright\!S),(f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)\times(f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)(x)(k\!\!\upharpoonright\!S\times S)(x,\cdot)\rangle_{l\times k,S}\,dP(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{S}\langle(f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)\times(h\!\!\upharpoonright\!S),((l\times k)\!\!\upharpoonright\!S\times S)(x,\cdot)\rangle_{l\times k,S}\,dP(x)

\displaystyle=\langle(f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)\times(h\!\!\upharpoonright\!S),\mathfrak{m}_{l\times k,S}\rangle_{l\times k,S}.

The key observation is now the following, for any
h\in\mathcal{H}
and almost surely

\displaystyle\langle h,f_{0}(X)k(X,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle=(h\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)(X)(f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)(X)-\langle(f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)\times(h\!\!\upharpoonright\!S),\mathfrak{m}_{l\times k,S}\rangle_{l\times k,S}

\displaystyle=\langle(f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)\times(h\!\!\upharpoonright\!S),\kappa(X,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{l\times k,S}\rangle_{l\times k,S}.

This leads directly to a first result. Under suitable assumptions and with
\delta=\bar{\lambda}_{\star}/2\|(f_{0}\otimes f_{0})\times k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}
,
\gamma=\delta/4
and
c=-\delta/2
, Equation (
5
) shows that for any
h\in\mathcal{H}
, such that
\|h\!\!\upharpoonright\!S\|_{S}=1
,

\displaystyle E(\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,f_{0}(X)k(X,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c))

\displaystyle=E(\psi_{\gamma}(\langle(f_{0}\!\!\upharpoonright\!S)\times(h\!\!\upharpoonright\!S),\kappa(X,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{l\times k,S}\rangle_{l\times k,S}-c))

\displaystyle\geq\bar{\lambda}_{\star}^{2}/8\|(f_{0}\otimes f_{0})\times k\|_{\infty}.

(31)

Combining the various steps above leads to the following proposition, which is an adaptation of Theorem
2
.

Proposition 2
.

Let
(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R},\mathcal{A},P)
be some probability space, let
P
be a topological measure that is
\tau
-additive, and
let
k
be a measurable kernel function defined on
\mathcal{X}
s.t. the corresponding RKHS
\mathcal{H}
is finite dimensional. Furthermore, let
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})
be i.i.d. random variables attaining values in
\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}
, with law
P
, and of the form
Y_{i}=f_{0}(X_{i})+\epsilon_{i}
where
\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}
are centered i.i.d. random variables which are independent of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
and such that
