|\epsilon_{1}|\leq c_{\epsilon}
(a.s.), and
f_{0}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(P)
. Assume that
\|(f_{0}\otimes f_{0})\times k\|_{\infty}<\infty
, and that the centered covariance operator
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c,l\times k}
has an eigen-decomposition with smallest non-zero eigenvalue being
\bar{\lambda}_{\star}
. Let
\delta=\bar{\lambda}_{\star}/2\|(f_{0}\otimes f_{0})\times k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}
and
p=\Pr(\epsilon_{1}\geq 0)\wedge\Pr(\epsilon_{1}\leq 0)
. For any
q\in(0,1)
and whenever
n
is (strictly) greater than

\displaystyle\frac{1024\|f_{0}\otimes f_{0}\times k\|^{2}_{\infty}}{p^{2}\bar{\lambda}_{\star}^{4}\delta^{2}}\Bigl(16(8\|f_{0}\otimes f_{0}\times k\|_{\infty}+6\sqrt{2\log(3/q)})^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\kern 5.0pt\vee 16(4c_{\epsilon}\|k\|_{\infty}\log(6/q)+((4c^{2}_{\epsilon}\|k\|^{2}_{\infty}+\sqrt{1+c^{2}_{\epsilon}\|k\|_{\infty}^{2}})\log(6/q))^{1/2})^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\kern 5.0pt\vee(\delta\sqrt{2\log(6/q)}+16\sqrt{2\pi}((\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+c_{\epsilon})\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}+\delta/2)+32\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}(\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+c_{\epsilon}))^{2}\Bigr)

then, with probability at least
1-q
, there exists a ball of radius
\delta/4
around
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
in
C_{y,n}
within the affine subspace spanned by
C_{y,n}
.

Proof.

(a)
Since
f_{0}\otimes f_{0}\times k
is a bounded kernel function we can apply (
3.3.1
)
and conclude for
\gamma=\delta/4
and
c=-\delta/2
that
E(\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,f_{0}(X)k(X,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c))\geq\bar{\lambda}_{\star}^{2}/8\|(f_{0}\otimes f_{0})\times k\|_{\infty}
and from (
3.3.1
) it follows that

E(\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,Yk(X,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c))\geq p\bar{\lambda}_{\star}^{2}/8\|(f_{0}\otimes f_{0})\times k\|_{\infty}.

(b)
Next, we have to incorporate a few triangle inequalities. First, we fill in the details in (
3.3.1
). From (
3.3.1
)
it follows that for
p_{1}\in(0,1)
and
\alpha_{1}=8n^{-1/2}\|f_{0}\otimes f_{0}\times k\|_{\infty}+6\sqrt{2\log(1/p_{1})}n^{-1/2}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\Bigl(\sup_{h\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}Pf_{0}\times h-P_{n}f_{0}\times h\geq\alpha_{1}/2\Bigr)=\Pr\Bigl(\sup_{g\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{l\times k}}|Pg-P_{n}g|\geq\alpha_{1}/2\Bigr)\leq p_{1}.

Also note that we can simplify (
25
) to

\Pr\big(\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\phi(X_{i})\|\geq\frac{\alpha_{2}}{2}\big)\leq 2\exp\Bigl(-\frac{n\alpha_{2}^{2}}{4c_{\epsilon}\|k\|_{\infty}(c_{\epsilon}\|k\|_{\infty}+\alpha_{2}+\sqrt{1+c_{\epsilon}^{2}\|k\|_{\infty}^{2}})}\Bigr),

whenever
n\geq\alpha_{2}c_{\epsilon}\|k\|_{\infty}
. Hence, for such
n
, for
p_{2}\in(0,1)
and

\alpha_{2}=\frac{4c_{\epsilon}\|k\|_{\infty}\log(2/p_{2})}{n}+\left(\frac{(4c^{2}_{\epsilon}\|k\|^{2}_{\infty}+\sqrt{1+c^{2}_{\epsilon}\|k\|_{\infty}^{2}})\log(2/p_{2})}{n}\right)^{1/2}

it follows that

\Pr\big(\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\phi(X_{i})\|\geq\frac{\alpha_{2}}{2}\big)\leq p_{2}.

In particular, for
\alpha_{12}=\alpha_{1}\vee\alpha_{2}
and whenever
n\geq\alpha_{2}c_{\epsilon}\|k\|_{\infty}
,

\Pr(\|\mathfrak{m}_{y}-\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}\|\geq\alpha_{12})\leq p_{1}+p_{2}.

(c)
From (
27
) we can infer that almost surely

\displaystyle P\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,yk(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c)-P_{n}\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,\tilde{y}k(\tilde{x},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}\rangle-c)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{-1}\|\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\|+P\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,yk(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c)-P_{n}\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,\tilde{y}k(\tilde{x},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c).

The same inequality holds almost surely if we consider the supremum over
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
. Therefore,
with
\gamma=\delta/4
and
\alpha>0
,

\displaystyle\Pr(\sup_{h\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}P\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,yk(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c)-P_{n}\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,\tilde{y}k(\tilde{x},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}\rangle-c)\geq 2\alpha)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr(\|\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\|\geq\delta\alpha/4)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\Pr(\sup_{h\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}P\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,yk(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c)-P_{n}\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,\tilde{y}k(\tilde{x},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c)\geq\alpha).

The latter term can be dealt with by a Rademacher argument when using (
3.3.1
). In detail, for any
p_{3}\in(0,1)
, with probability

1-p_{3}
simultaneously for all
h\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
,

\displaystyle P_{n}\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,\tilde{y}k(\tilde{x},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c)\geq

\displaystyle P\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,yk(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c)-\sqrt{2\log(2/p_{3})/n}

\displaystyle\quad-2E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{h\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}\bigl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}\psi_{\gamma}(Y_{i}h(X_{i})-E(Yh(X))-c)\bigr|\bigr)

follows from
[
19
, Thm3.4.5]
; also see p.
2.2
. Substituting (
3.3.1
) leads to the following lower bound on the
P_{n}
term,

\displaystyle P\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,yk(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c)-\sqrt{2\log(2/p_{3})/n}

\displaystyle-(4/\gamma)(\|Y\|_{\infty}E_{\zeta}\bigl(\sup_{h\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}\bigl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}h(X_{i})\bigr|\bigr)+(\|Y\|_{\infty}\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}+|c|)\sqrt{2\pi/n}).

Filling in
\gamma
,
c
, the upper bound on
|Y|
and the Rademacher complexity of
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
, reduces the lower bound to

\alpha_{3}=\frac{\sqrt{2\log(2/p_{3})}}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{16\sqrt{2\pi}((\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+c_{\epsilon})\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}+\delta/2)}{\delta\sqrt{n}}+\frac{32\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}(\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+c_{\epsilon})}{\delta\sqrt{n}}

and

\displaystyle\Pr\bigl(\sup_{h\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}P\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,yk(x,\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c)-P_{n}\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,\tilde{y}k(\tilde{x},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y}\rangle-c)\geq\alpha_{3}\bigr)\leq p_{3}.

(d)
Combining these bounds we can derive a lower bound on
P_{n}\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,\tilde{y}k(\tilde{x},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}\rangle-c)
.
In detail, let
p_{1}=p_{2}=p_{3}=q/3
and set

\alpha^{\star}=\frac{4\alpha_{12}}{\delta}\vee\alpha_{3}

then with probability
1-q
simultaneously for all
h\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
,

P_{n}\psi_{\gamma}(\langle h,\tilde{y}k(\tilde{x},\cdot)-\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}\rangle-c)\geq p\bar{\lambda}_{\star}^{2}/8\|(f_{0}\otimes f_{0})\times k\|_{\infty}-2\alpha^{\star}.

To guarantee that the right hand side is strictly positive we can choose the
n
which is provided in the statement of the proposition.
∎

3.3.2
Second setting: Bounded
Y_{i}
’s & Eq. (
22
)

In this section we map
h\in\mathcal{H}
to
\check{h}=\langle 1,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes h(\cdot)
and we work with the kernel function

\rho((y_{1},x_{1}),(y_{2},x_{2}))=\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}k(x_{1},x_{2}).

In the introduction to this section we denoted the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
by
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{H}
. We will use in the following the more compact notation
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
,
\|\cdot\|_{\rho}
etc.

Compared to the approach in the previous section, using
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
offers a dramatic simplification of the analysis and leads to improved bounds.
As usual, we are interested in approximating a mean element. In the current context, this is
\mathfrak{m}^{\otimes}_{y}=\int\rho((y,x),\cdot)\,dP(x,y)
and
there is a straight forward relation to the element
\mathfrak{m}_{y}
that was used in the previous section,

\mathfrak{m}^{\otimes}_{y}=\int\langle y,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes k(x,\cdot)\,dP(x,y)=\langle 1,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes\int yk(x,\cdot)\,dP(x,y)=\langle 1,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{y},

using (
2
). The element
\mathfrak{m}^{\otimes}_{y}
lies in
{\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
whenever
Y^{2}k(X,X)\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mu)
.
Under our assumption that
Y=f_{0}(X)+\epsilon
,
X
and
\epsilon
independent random variables, the representation of
\mathfrak{m}_{y}^{\otimes}
simplifies to

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{y}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=E(\langle f_{0}(X)+\epsilon,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes k(X,\cdot))

\displaystyle=E(f_{0}(X)\langle 1,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes k(X,\cdot))+\langle E(\epsilon),\cdot\rangle\otimes E(k(X,\cdot))

\displaystyle=\langle 1,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes E(f_{0}(X)k(X,\cdot)),

where we used (
2
) in the second and in the last equality. This is again just
\langle 1,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{y}
.

If
P
is
\tau
-additive as a measure on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}
then the support
S
of
P
is well defined and we have a natural population limit

C^{\otimes}_{y}=\text{{cch}\,}\{\langle y,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes k(x,\cdot):(x,y)\in S\}

of the empirical convex set

C^{\otimes}_{y,n}=\text{{cch}\,}\{\langle Y_{i},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes k(X_{i},\cdot):i\leq n\}.

These are just the convex sets associated with the kernel function
\rho
acting on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{X}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{y}^{\otimes},\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}
are the corresponding mean and empirical mean elements. In fact, we can apply right away Theorem
2
. Our sample space is then
[-\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}-c_{\epsilon},\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+c_{\epsilon}]\times\mathcal{X}
,
where
c_{\epsilon}
is a constant such that
|Y|\leq c_{\epsilon}
a.s. The kernel function is
\rho
restricted to the sample space and

\|\rho\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\times S\|_{\infty}\leq(\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+c_{\epsilon})\|k\|_{\infty}.

In this formulation it might not be directly obvious how assumptions on the distribution of
Y
enter. Using a Rademacher argument we can control the difference between
P
and
P_{n}
when acting on indicator functions. To do so we do do not need any assumption on the distribution of
Y
beside boundedness. But, if you recall our earlier arguments, you will notice that we used lower bounds on
P
when applied to indicator functions to control the size of
C^{\otimes}_{y,n}
. This lower bound on
P
depends on the distribution of
Y
. In particular, with the covariance operator approach, it depends on the variance of
Y
.

Let
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes}_{c}
be the centered covariance operator corresponding to kernel
\rho
then for
g,h\in\mathcal{H}
,

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes}_{c}\check{g},\check{h}\rangle_{\rho}

\displaystyle=E(Y^{2}g(X)h(X))-E(Yg(X))E(Yh(X))

\displaystyle=E(f_{0}^{2}(X)g(X)h(X))+\sigma^{2}E(g(X)h(X))-E(f_{0}(X)g(X))E(f_{0}(X)h(X)),

where
\sigma^{2}
is the variance of
\epsilon
. We can also relate this expression back to the covariance operator discussed in the earlier approach, for
h\in\mathcal{H}
,

\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes}_{c}\check{g},\check{h}\rangle_{\rho}=\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c,l\times k}f_{0}\otimes g,f_{0}\otimes h\rangle_{l\times k}+\sigma^{2}E(g(X))E(h(X)).

Recall that for
g,h\in\mathcal{H}
,
\langle g,h\rangle=\langle f_{0}\times g,f_{0}\times h\rangle_{l\times k}
and note that
\langle\check{g},\check{h}\rangle_{\rho}=\langle g,h\rangle
. In particular,

\|h\|=\|\check{h}\|_{\rho}=\|f_{0}\times h\|_{l\times k}

(32)

for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
. If
h\in\mathcal{H},\|h\|=1
, is such that
f_{0}\times h
is an eigenfunction of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c,l\times k}
with eigenvalue
\lambda
and
\sigma^{2}
is the variance of
\epsilon
then

\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes}_{c}\check{h},\check{h}\rangle_{\rho}=\lambda+\sigma^{2}E(h^{2}(X)).

Furthermore, if
f_{0}\times h
is an eigenfunction of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c,l\times k}
and
g
is such that
\langle g,h\rangle=0
then

\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes}_{c}\check{h},\check{g}\rangle_{\rho}=\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c,l\times k}f_{0}\times h,f_{0}\times g\rangle_{l\times k}+\sigma^{2}E(g(X))E(h(X))=\sigma^{2}E(g(X))E(h(X)).

There is no reason why the latter term should be zero and the two operators will generally not have the same eigenfunctions (in the sense that
\check{h}
is an eigenfunction of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes}_{c}
iff
f_{0}\otimes h
is an eigenfunction of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c,l\times k}
).

Remark 1
.

If
v=\inf\{E(h^{2}(X)):h\in\mathcal{H},\|h\|=1\}>0
then
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes}_{c}
has no eigenvalue below
\sigma^{2}v
. This can help with the compression, but notice that larger values of
\sigma^{2}
are related to larger values
\|\rho\|_{\infty}
which hinders the compression.

Lower bounds on the width.

We could also look at the width of the convex set
C_{y}^{\otimes}
by means of the kernel function
\rho
. While this is a useful exercise we only want to highlight here a simple relation between the width of
C_{y}^{\otimes}
and the width of usual convex set
C_{S}
(as a subset of
\mathcal{H}_{S}
). For
h\in\mathcal{H}
,
\|h\|=1
,

\displaystyle\text{{width}\,}_{\check{h}}(C_{y}^{\otimes})

\displaystyle=\sup_{(x,y)\in S}(y-f_{0}(x))\langle h,k(x,\cdot)\rangle+h(x)f_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-\infd_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in S}((y^{\prime}-f_{0}(x^{\prime}))\langle h,k(x^{\prime},\cdot)\rangle+h(x^{\prime})f_{0}(x^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{S}}\sup_{y,y^{\prime}\in S_{x}}(y-y^{\prime})h(x)=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{S}}\sup_{y,y^{\prime}\in S_{x}}(y-y^{\prime})|h(x)|,

where
\mathcal{X}_{S}=\{x:(x,y)\in S\}
and
S_{x}=\{y:(x,y)\in S\}
. Also, note that

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{S}}|h(x)|\geq(1/2)\text{{width}\,}_{h}(C_{S})

and we have a lower bound on
\text{{width}\,}_{\check{h}}(C_{y}^{\otimes})
which is a product of the width of
C_{S}
and the spread of
\epsilon
.

3.3.3
Third setting: Unbounded
Y_{i}
’s & Eq. (
22
)

If
Y
is unbounded
then one way to approach the approximation problem is to cap the observations
Y_{i}
and to control the cap as a function of
n
. We demonstrate this for the case that we have the model stated in (
23
),

f_{0}
is
bounded
and
measurable
, and
\epsilon
is
sub-Gaussian
with variance factor
\nu
(see
[
GAB13
, Sec.2.3]
) but is not necessarily bounded. The natural sample space is now
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{X}
. The kernel
\rho
is well defined on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{X}
but is unbounded. When
f_{0}
is bounded,
k
is measurable and bounded, and
\epsilon
is sub-Gaussian, we have that
\langle Y,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}k(X,\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mu,\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
since

E(\|\langle Y,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}k(X,\cdot)\|_{\rho}^{2})=E(Y^{2}k(X,X))\leq\|k\|_{\infty}E(Y^{2})

and the latter term is finite since
Y
is sub-Gaussian.
Let
\{r_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
be a non-negative and non-decreasing sequence, and let
\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}=(Y\wedge(r_{n}+\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}))\vee-(r_{n}+\|f_{0}\|_{\infty})
. The
\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}
can obviously be replaced by an upper bound on the norm, but as the argument is developed such a bound is needed to control the error introduced by capping the observations
Y_{i}
. Define
\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}_{y,n}^{\otimes}=(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}_{i},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}k(X_{i},\cdot)
. In this section our aim is to derive a suitable adapted version of Theorem
2
for this setting where
\epsilon
is sub-Gaussian. We start by investigating the effect of the capping of
Y
.

Bounding
\|\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}-\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}_{y,n}^{\otimes}\|
.

A simple expansion yields

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}-\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}_{y,n}^{\otimes}\|^{2}_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle Y_{i}-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}_{i},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}k(X_{i},\cdot)\|_{\rho}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}_{i})(Y_{j}-\wideparen{Y}_{j}^{(n)})k(X_{i},X_{j}).

Due to the independence of the observations and by using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle E(\|\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}-\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}_{y,n}^{\otimes}\|^{2}_{\rho})\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|E(Y_{i}-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}_{i})^{2}k(X_{i},X_{i})|

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\not=j}|\langle E((Y_{i}-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}_{i})k(X_{i},\cdot)),E((Y_{j}-\wideparen{Y}_{j}^{(n)})k(X_{j},\cdot))\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\|k\|_{\infty}}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E(Y_{i}-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}_{i})^{2}+\frac{n-1}{n}E^{2}(|Y_{1}-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}_{1}|\|k(X_{1},\cdot)\|)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\|k\|_{\infty}}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E(Y_{i}-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}_{i})^{2}+\frac{(n-1)\|k\|_{\infty}}{n}E^{2}(|Y_{1}-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}_{1}|).

Let
\lambda
denote the law of
\epsilon
. Noting that
\epsilon
is sub-Gaussian and using
[
GAB13
, Sec.2.3]
together with
[
18
, 252O]
and
[
13
, Prop2.5(a)]
, we obtain

\displaystyle E(|Y_{i}-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}_{i}|)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}(t-r_{n})\times\chi\{t\geq r_{n}\}\,d\lambda(t)+\int_{-\infty}^{0}(-t-r_{n})\times\chi\{-t\geq r_{n}\}\,d\lambda(t)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda\{t:t\geq s+r_{n}\}\,ds+\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda\{t:-t\geq s+r_{n}\}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{r_{n}}^{\infty}e^{-s^{2}/2\nu}\,ds\leq\sqrt{2\pi\nu}e^{-r_{n}^{2}/2\nu}.

(33)

Similarly,

\displaystyle E(Y_{i}-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}_{i})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}(t-r_{n})^{2}\times\chi\{t\geq r_{n}\}\,d\lambda(t)+\int_{-\infty}^{0}(-t-r_{n})^{2}\times\chi\{-t\geq r_{n}\}\,d\lambda(t)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda\{t:t\geq\sqrt{s}+r_{n}\}\,ds+\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda\{t:t\leq-\sqrt{s}-r_{n}\}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-r_{n}^{2}/2\nu}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-s/2\nu}\,ds=4\nu e^{-r_{n}^{2}/2\nu}.

(34)

Combining these yields

E(\|\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}-\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}_{y,n}^{\otimes}\|^{2}_{\rho})\leq 2\nu\|k\|_{\infty}e^{-r_{n}^{2}/2\nu}\Bigl(2/n+\pi e^{-r_{n}^{2}/2\nu}\Bigr)

and

\displaystyle\Pr(\|\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}-\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}_{y,n}^{\otimes}\|_{\rho}\geq t)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\nu\|k\|_{\infty}e^{-r_{n}^{2}/2\nu}\Bigl(2/n+\pi e^{-r_{n}^{2}/2\nu}\Bigr)}{t^{2}}.

In other words, if
we have an upper bound on
\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}
and cap the observations as described above then
with probability
1-\delta
for any
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\|\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}-\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}_{y,n}^{\otimes}\|_{\rho}\leq\sqrt{2\nu}\|k\|^{1/2}_{\infty}e^{-r_{n}^{2}/4\nu}\Bigl(2/n+\pi e^{-r_{n}^{2}/2\nu}\Bigr)^{1/2}\delta^{-1/2}.

(35)

Spectrum of the covariance operator.

In Remark
1
we observed that no eigenvalue of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes}_{c}
can be lower than
\sigma^{2}v
, where
\sigma^{2}
is the variance of
\epsilon
and
v=\inf\{E(h^{2}(X)):h\in\mathcal{H},\|h\|=1\}
. There is a peculiar detail that we have to be careful about: if
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
are linearly independent but
h_{1}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\mathcal{X}_{S}=h_{2}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\mathcal{X}_{S}
, where
\mathcal{X}_{S}=\{x:(x,y)\in S\}
, then there exists an
h\in\mathcal{H}
of norm one for which
E(h^{2}(X))=0
. Furthermore, the corresponding functions
\check{h}_{1},\check{h}_{2}
in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
are not constant on
S
but some linear combination of
\check{h}_{1}
and
\check{h}_{2}
is zero on
S
(at least when the involved functions are continuous). To make use of the lower bound
\sigma^{2}v
it makes therefore sense to move right away to functions restricted to
S_{f}=\{(y,x):(x,y)\in S\}
or
\mathcal{X}_{S}
.

Before coming back to the lower bound we want to take a paragraph to understand better
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,S_{f}}
, which is the RKHS corresponding to the kernel function
\rho\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}\times S_{f}
. First, notice that there are no (non-zero) constant functions in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,S_{f}}
if
\epsilon
is not almost surely zero. In particular, the covariance operator
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}^{\otimes,S}
has then only eigenvalues that are strictly positive. If
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}^{\otimes}
has zero eigenvalues then there must be elements
\check{h}
which are constant on
S
but this means that these elements have to be equal to zero on
S
and correspond to the origin in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,S_{f}}
.
Also notice that
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,S_{f}}
is not the same RKHS as the RKHS with kernel function
(l\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\mathbb{R}_{S}\times\mathbb{R}_{S})\times(k\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\mathcal{X}_{S}\times\mathcal{X}_{S})
, where
\mathbb{R}_{S}=\{y:(x,y)\in S\}
and
l(y,y^{\prime})=\langle y,y^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}
for all
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
. This follows directly since they have different domains. The latter kernel is defined for pairs
(x,y)
in
\mathcal{X}_{S}\times\mathbb{R}_{S}
while the former is defined for pairs
(x,y)\in S
. This is inconvenient since we like to use
E(h^{2}(X))
for
h
in some RKHS of functions acting on the support of some measure and it is not directly
obvious what this support should be like. In the following, let
\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{X}}
be a topology on
\mathcal{X}
, let
\mathfrak{T}
be the corresponding product topology on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}
, and assume that the law
P
of
(X,Y)
is a
Radon measure
with
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
; in particular, it is a topological
\tau
-additive measure and
\mathfrak{T}\subset\mathcal{A}
. Then
S
is well defined as a subset of
\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}
. Let us also introduce our probability space
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mu)
, assume that
\mu
is
complete
and
(X,Y)
is a well defined random variable in the sense that
(X,Y)^{-1}[A]\in\Sigma
for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
.
Furthermore, consider the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{X}}=\{A:A\times\mathbb{R}\in\mathcal{A}\}
and let
P_{X}=P\circ\pi_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}
where the function
\pi_{\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{X}
projects onto the first coordinate. The
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{X}}
contains
\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{X}}
since for
O\in\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{X}}
it holds that
O\times\mathbb{R}
is in the product topology
\mathfrak{T}
.
Hence,
P_{X}
is a topological measure. If
\mathcal{X}
is a
Hausdorff space
then
P_{X}
is, in fact, a Radon measure (apply
[
18
, 418I]
to
\pi_{\mathcal{X}}
and note that
\pi_{\mathcal{X}}
is continuous). This implies that the support
S^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{X}
of
P_{X}
is well defined. Observe that
\overline{\mathcal{X}_{S}}=S^{\prime}
: the projection
\pi_{\mathcal{X}}
is a continuous inverse-measure preserving function from
\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}
to
\mathcal{X}
and, due to
[
18
, 411N.e]
, the support of
P_{\mathcal{X}}
is

\overline{\pi_{\mathcal{X}}[S]}=\overline{\{x:(x,y)\in S\}}=\overline{S_{\mathcal{X}}}
. We also have to check that
X
is actually a well defined random variable in the sense that

X^{-1}[A]\in\Sigma
for all
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{X}}
, and that
E(h^{2}(X))=\int h^{2}\,dP_{X}=\int(h\!\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\overline{\mathcal{X}_{S}})^{2}\,dP_{X}
. The former can be seen in the following way. Since
\pi_{\mathcal{X}}
is a measurable function from
(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R},\mathcal{A})
to
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{X}})
it follows that
X=\pi_{\mathcal{X}}\circ(X,Y)
is measurable as a mapping from
(\Omega,\Sigma)
to
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{X}})
and is a well defined random variable. For the latter, if
h
is in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathcal{X},P_{X})
then
\int h^{2}\,dP_{X}
is well defined and obviously equal to
\int(h\!\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\!\overline{\mathcal{X}_{S}})^{2}\,dP_{X}
. It remains to show the
E(h^{2}(X))=\int h^{2}\,dP_{X}
. One way to show this is to use
[
18
, 235E]
. This can be applied since
\mu X^{-1}[A]=\Pr(X\in A,Y\in\mathbb{R})=P\pi_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}[A]=P_{\mathcal{X}}(A)
for any
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

We can now define an RKHS of functions that act on the support
\overline{\mathcal{X}_{S}}
. For compactness of notation let
\tilde{S}=\overline{\mathcal{X}_{S}}
,
let
k_{\tilde{S}}=k\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S}\!\times\!\tilde{S}
and denote the corresponding RKHS by
\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{S}}
. This RKHS allows us to carry over Remark
1
to the case where we work with
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,S_{f}}
. Let
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes,S}_{c}
be the centered covariance operator that corresponds to
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,S_{f}}
. Since,

\mathcal{H}_{\rho,S_{f}}=\{\check{h}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}:h\in\mathcal{H}\}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes,S}_{c}\check{h}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f},\check{h}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}\rangle_{\rho,S_{f}}

\displaystyle=E(f^{2}_{0}(X)h^{2}(X))+\sigma^{2}E(h^{2}(X))-E^{2}(f_{0}(X)h(X))

\displaystyle=E(f_{0}^{2}(X)h^{2}(X))+\sigma^{2}\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\tilde{S}}h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S},h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S}\rangle_{\tilde{S}}-E^{2}(f_{0}(X)h(X)).

(36)

When the kernel function
k
is
continuous
, then the only function
h\in\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{S}}
for which
E(h^{2}(X))=0
is
h=0
which has norm zero (otherwise there is an open set on which
h^{2}(X)
is bounded away from zero and the intersection of this open set with the support has measure strictly larger than zero
[
18
, 411N]
). In this case,
all the eigenvalues of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\tilde{S}}
are strictly positive and also all eigenvalues of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes,S}_{c}
are strictly positive, implying that there is no constant function in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho,S_{f}}
. Also, note that
\|h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S}\|_{\tilde{S}}=\|\check{h}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}\|_{\rho,S_{f}}
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
: first observe that
\check{g}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}=\check{h}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}
if, and only if,
g\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S}=h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S}
. If

g\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S}=h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S}
then for
(x,y)\in S
,
\check{g}(y,x)=yg(x)=yh(x)=\check{h}(y,x)
because
x\in\mathcal{X}_{S}\subset\tilde{S}
. On the other hand, if
\check{g}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}=\check{h}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}
then for any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{S}
there exist a point
y
such that
(x,y)\in S
and
yg(x)=yh(x)
which implies
g(x)=h(x)
if
y\not=0
. In fact, if
\sigma^{2}>0
, there exist at least two such points and, in particular, there exists a
y\not=0
such that
yg(x)=yh(x)
. Since
g=h
on the dense subset
\mathcal{X}_{S}
of
\tilde{S}
and both
g,h
are continuous (assuming
k
is continuous) it follows that
g=h
on
\tilde{S}
(e.g.
[
15
, Thm1.5.4]
and using that
\mathbb{R}
is a Hausdorff space). Using (
32
),

\displaystyle\|\check{h}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}\|_{\rho,S_{f}}

\displaystyle=\inf\{\|\check{g}\|_{\rho}:\check{g}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}=\check{h}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f},g\in\mathcal{H}\}=\inf\{\|g\|:\check{g}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}=\check{h}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f},g\in\mathcal{H}\}

\displaystyle=\inf\{\|g\|:g\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S}=h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S},g\in\mathcal{H}\}=\|h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S}\|_{\tilde{S}}.

(37)

This implies that a strictly positive lower bound on the

\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\tilde{S}}h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S},h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S}\rangle_{\tilde{S}}
is given by the smallest eigenvalue of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\tilde{S}}

(when
\mathcal{H}
is finite dimensional). We can also express this bound in terms of
\mathcal{H}
since for
h\in\mathcal{H}
,
\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}h,h\rangle=\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\tilde{S}}h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S},h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S}\rangle_{\tilde{S}}
and
\|h\|\geq\|h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S}\|_{\tilde{S}}
it follows that the smallest eigenvalue of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}
provides a lower bound on the smallest eigenvalue of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\tilde{S}}
and this lower bound is strictly positive. We might be tempted to improve this lower bound by recalling
that the eigenvalues of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}
tell us the dimension of
\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{S}}
, but notice that there is no reason why the eigenfunctions of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}
should be related since one corresponds to the variance and the other corresponds to the second moment, and it is not directly obvious of how to benefit from the additional information that
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c}
provides.

A family of covariance operators.

The move from
Y
to
\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}
affects the covariance, and the covariance operator corresponding to
\langle Y,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}k(X,\cdot)
is not the same as the covariance operator corresponding to
\langle\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}k(X,\cdot)
,
n\geq 1
. Let us denote the covariance operators corresponding to the
\wideparen{Y}
’s by the somewhat unwieldy
\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{\otimes,(n)}_{c}
and the covariance operator corresponding to the support
S_{n}
of the law of
(X,\wideparen{Y}^{(n)})
by
\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{\otimes,S_{n}}_{c}
; we assume that the laws
P^{(n)}
of
(X,\wideparen{Y}^{(n)})
are
Radon measures
, which guarantees that
the support of
P^{(n)}
is well defined
. It is easy to verify that

P^{(n)}
is a Radon measure if
P
itself is a Radon measure
and the topology corresponding to
P
is a Hausdorff topology.
Consider the set
A=\mathcal{X}\times[-(r_{n}+\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}),r_{n}+\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}]
equipped with the subspace topology which is also a Hausdorff topology
[
15
, Thm.2.1.6]
, and the continuous function
f:\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}\to A
given by
f(x,y)=(x,\wideparen{y}^{(n)})
, where we mean the same transformation as for the random variable
Y
. The push-forward
P^{(n)}=f_{\#}P
is a Radon measure according to
[
18
, 418I]
.

We need lower bounds on the smallest non-zero eigenvalues of the different
\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{\otimes,(n)}_{c}
operators to use our compression approach.
It seems natural to
work with an assumption on the smallest eigenvalue of the covariance operator
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes,S}_{c}
, which corresponds to the original
Y
, and to relate the eigenvalues of
\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{\otimes,S_{n}}_{c}
back to the eigenvalues of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes,S}_{c}
. As discussed on the previous page,
the covariance operator
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes,S}_{c}
does not have an eigenvalue that is zero if
k
is continuous
. In this case, the smallest eigenvalue
\wideparen{\lambda}^{(n)}_{\star}
of
\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{\otimes,S_{n}}_{c}
is at least of size
\bar{\lambda}_{\star}/2
, where
\bar{\lambda}_{\star}
is the smallest eigenvalue of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes,S}_{c}
, whenever

\|\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes,S}_{c}-\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{\otimes,S_{n}}_{c}\|_{op}\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}v}{2},

where (
36
) tells us that we can choose
v
either as the smallest eigenvalue of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\tilde{S}}
or the smallest non-zero eigenvalue of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}
, and where
0<\sigma^{2}\leq\nu
is the variance of
\epsilon
. Alternatively, we can obviously also directly impose assumptions on the eigenvalues of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes,S}_{c}
. We can bound the operator norm in the following way,

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes,S}_{c}-\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{\otimes,S_{n}}_{c}\|_{op}=\sup_{\|\check{h}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f}\|_{\rho,S_{f}}=1}\sup_{\|\check{g}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f}\|_{\rho,S_{f}}=1\vphantom{\|\check{h}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f}\|_{\rho,S_{f}}=1}}\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes,S}_{c}\check{h}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}-\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{\otimes,S_{n}}_{c}\check{h}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f},\check{g}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}\rangle_{\rho,S_{f}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|h\!\upharpoonright\!\tilde{S}\|_{\tilde{S}}=1}\sup_{\|g\!\upharpoonright\!\tilde{S}\|_{\tilde{S}}=1}E((Y^{2}-(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)})^{2})h(X)g(X))-E(Yg(X))E(Yh(X))

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+E(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}g(X))E(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}h(X)).

Let us first address the second moment term. For
h,g
such that
\|h\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S}\|_{\tilde{S}}=1=\|g\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S}\|_{\tilde{S}}
,

\displaystyle|E((Y^{2}-(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)})^{2})h(X)g(X))|\leq\|k\|_{\infty}E(Y^{2}-(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)})^{2})

\displaystyle=\|k\|_{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}(t^{2}-r_{n}^{2})\times\chi\{t\geq r_{n}\}\,d\lambda(t)+\|k\|_{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{0}(t^{2}-r_{n}^{2})\times\chi\{-t\geq r_{n}\}\,d\lambda(t)

\displaystyle\leq\|k\|_{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda\{t:t\geq\sqrt{s+r_{n}^{2}}\}\,ds+\|k\|_{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda\{t:t\leq-\sqrt{s+r_{n}^{2}}\}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq 2\|k\|_{\infty}\int_{r_{n}^{2}}^{\infty}e^{-s/2\nu}\,ds=4\nu\|k\|_{\infty}e^{-r^{2}_{n}/2\nu}.

The other term can be controlled in the following way,

\displaystyle|E(Yg(X))E(Yh(X))-E(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}g(X))E(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}h(X))|

\displaystyle\leq|E((Y-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)})g(X))E(f_{0}(X)h(X))|+|E(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}g(X))E((Y-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)})h(X))|

\displaystyle\leq\|k\|_{\infty}(2\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+r_{n})E(|Y-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}|)\leq\sqrt{8\pi\nu}\|k\|_{\infty}(2\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+r_{n})e^{-r_{n}^{2}/2\nu}.

Combining these yields

\|\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes,S}_{c}-\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{\otimes,S_{n}}_{c}\|_{op}\leq(\sqrt{8\pi\nu}(2\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+r_{n})+4\nu)\|k\|_{\infty}e^{-r_{n}^{2}/2\nu}.

(38)

In particular, if we use
\nu=\sigma^{2}
,

r_{1}=(1\vee 2\sigma^{2})\vee 2\sigma\log^{1/2}\Bigl(\frac{\|k\|_{\infty}(12\sqrt{\pi}(\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+1)+8\sigma)}{\sigma\bar{\lambda}_{\star,\tilde{S}}}\Bigr),

(39)

where
\bar{\lambda}_{\star,\tilde{S}}
is the smallest non-zero eigenvalue of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\tilde{S}}
, and let
\{r_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
be a non-decreasing sequence then
for all
n\geq 1
,

\wideparen{\lambda}_{\star}^{(n)}\geq\bar{\lambda}_{\star,\tilde{S}}/2.

This follows from the argument on the last page and because this choice guarantees that the right hand side of Equation (
38
)
is upper bounded by
\sigma^{2}\bar{\lambda}_{\star,\tilde{S}}
: first notice that
r_{1}^{2}/4\sigma^{2}\geq\log r_{1}
for any
r_{1}\geq 1\vee 2\sigma^{2}
. Hence,

\displaystyle(\sqrt{32\pi}\sigma\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+4\sigma^{2})\|k\|_{\infty}e^{-r_{1}^{2}/2\sigma^{2}}+\sqrt{8\pi}\sigma\|k\|_{\infty}e^{-r_{1}^{2}/2\sigma^{2}+\log r_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq(\sqrt{32\pi}\sigma\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+4\sigma^{2})\|k\|_{\infty}e^{-r_{1}^{2}/2\sigma^{2}}+\sqrt{8\pi}\sigma\|k\|_{\infty}e^{-r_{1}^{2}/4\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq(\sqrt{32\pi}\sigma(\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+1)+4\sigma^{2})\|k\|_{\infty}e^{-r_{1}^{2}/4\sigma^{2}}.

The same arguments applies to any
r_{n}>r_{1}
and the final display is non-increasing in the
r_{1}
argument. Setting this final display equal to
\sigma^{2}\bar{\lambda}_{\star,\tilde{S}}/2
yields the expression in (
39
).

Also, notice that
r_{1}
depends logarithmically on the unknown terms
\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}
and
\bar{\lambda}_{\star,\tilde{S}}
.

Compression in the case of sub-Gaussian noise

We have now all the ingredients to state a proposition for the
sub-Gaussian noise case under the assumption that we have an upper bound on
\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}
, a lower bound on
\bar{\lambda}_{\star,\tilde{S}}
and some control over the variance term
\sigma^{2}
. In particular, we know that when

r_{1}
is chosen as in (
39
)
that
\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{\otimes,S_{n}}_{c}
has eigenvalues that are closely related to the eigenvalues of
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes,S}_{c}
and that we can apply our results to compress

\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}_{y,n}^{\otimes}
. Furthermore, we know how to control the difference between
\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}_{y,n}^{\otimes}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}
. The following proposition ties these results together.

Proposition 3
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{X}})
be a Hausdorff space,
(\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R},\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{A},P)
be a topological measure space such that
P
is a Radon probability measure which has support
S
,
\mathfrak{T}
is the product topology corresponding to
\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{X}}
and the standard topology on
\mathbb{R}
, and let
k
be a continuous and bounded kernel function defined on
\mathcal{X}
such that the corresponding RKHS
\mathcal{H}
is finite dimensional.
Furthermore, let
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})
be i.i.d. random variables attaining values in
\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}
, with law
P
, and of the form
Y_{i}=f_{0}(X_{i})+\epsilon_{i}
, where
\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}
are centered i.i.d. random variables which are independent of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
and such that
\epsilon_{1}
is sub-Gaussian with variance
0<\sigma^{2}
, and
f_{0}
is a measurable and bounded function. Let
\bar{\lambda}_{\star,\tilde{S}}
be the smallest eigenvalue of the covariance operator
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\tilde{S}}
that corresponds to the kernel function

k\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\tilde{S}\times\tilde{S}
, where
\tilde{S}
is the closure of
\{x:(x,y)\in S\}
in
\mathcal{X}
. Furthermore, let
\bar{\lambda}_{\star}
be the smallest eigenvalue of of the covariance operator
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes,S}_{c}
corresponding to the kernel function
\rho\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S\times S
,
\rho((y_{1},x_{1}),(y_{2},x_{2}))=y_{1}y_{2}k(x_{1},x_{2})
for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X},y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. Given
q\in(0,1)
define the sequence
\{r_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
in the following way. Define
r_{1}
as in (
39
)
and for
n\geq 2
through

r_{n}=r_{1}\vee\sqrt{2}\sigma\log(n/q).

Under these conditions, for any
n\geq 1
, the smallest eigenvalue
\wideparen{\lambda}_{\star}^{(n)}
of
\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{\otimes,S_{n}}_{c}
fulfills
\wideparen{\lambda}_{\star}^{(n)}\geq\bar{\lambda}_{\star}/2\geq\sigma^{2}\bar{\lambda}_{\star,\tilde{S}}/2>0
and there exists a ball of radius
\wideparen{\delta}^{(n)}=\wideparen{\lambda}_{\star}^{(n)}/(2(\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+r_{n})^{1/2}\|k\|^{1/2}_{\infty})
around
\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}^{\otimes}_{y}
within the affine space spanned by
\wideparen{C}_{\rho}=\{\rho((y,x),\cdot):(x,y)\in S_{n}\}
as a subset of
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
, where
S_{n}
is the support of the law of
P^{(n)}
corresponding to
(X,\wideparen{Y}^{(n)})
. Whenever
n
is (strictly) greater than

\displaystyle\left(\frac{8(\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+r_{n})\|k\|_{\infty}(\sqrt{2\log(12/q)}+192(\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+r_{n})\|k\|_{\infty}/\wideparen{\lambda}^{(n)}_{\star})}{(\wideparen{\lambda}^{(n)}_{\star})^{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\vee\left(\frac{16(\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+r_{n})^{1/2}\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}+\sqrt{288\log(4/q)}}{\wideparen{\delta}^{(n)}}\right)^{2}

with probability
1-q
there exists a ball of radius
\wideparen{\delta}^{(n)}/4
around
\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}^{\otimes}_{y,n}
in
\wideparen{C}^{\otimes}_{\rho,n}
within the affine subspace spanned by
\wideparen{C}_{\rho}
and

\|\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}^{\otimes}_{y,n}-\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}\|_{\rho}\leq 6\sigma\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}n^{-3/4}.

Proof.

We derived the inequalities concerning the eigenvalues earlier in this section. Furthermore, the bound on
\wideparen{\delta}^{(n)}
follows directly
when applying Theorem
2
to the random variables
(X,\wideparen{Y}^{(n)})
and the kernel function
\rho\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{n}\times S_{n}
, noting that
\|\rho\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{n}\times S_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq(\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+r_{n})\|k\|_{\infty}
. The bound on
n
is also taken from Theorem
2
with the only modification being that a union bound is used to guarantee simultaneously the existence of the ball around
\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}^{\otimes}_{y,n}
within
\wideparen{C}^{\otimes}_{\rho,n}
and that

\|\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}^{\otimes}_{y,n}-\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}\|_{\rho}
is upper bounded by
n^{-1/2}
. In detail, for the stated
q
with probability
1-q/2
there exists a ball around
\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}^{\otimes}_{y,n}
and with the given choice of
r_{n}
, with probability
1-q/2

\displaystyle\|\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}^{\otimes}_{y,n}-\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}\|_{\rho}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}}\|k\|^{1/2}_{\infty}e^{-r_{n}^{2}/4\sigma^{2}}\Bigl(2/n+\pi e^{-r_{n}^{2}/2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)^{1/2}(q/2)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sigma\|k\|^{1/2}_{\infty}n^{-1/2}n^{-1/4}(2n^{-1/2}+\pi)^{1/2}\leq 3\sigma\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}\pi^{1/2}n^{-3/4}.

follows from (
35
).

∎

3.4
Simultaneous compression

In this section we are interested in compressing different quantities like the covariance operator and the mean element simultaneously, meaning that we want to find
a single
convex combination of a subset of the data that allows us to approximate both quantities well. As mentioned in the introduction, we are utilizing a direct sum approach to approach the simultaneous compression problem. In this section, we start our exploration with
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
for bounded
Y
, which is in some sense easy to deal with since the RKHSs corresponding to them have intersection
\{0\}
(after some minor adjustments of the kernel functions) which makes the direct sum approach easy to apply. We then explore how we can deal with RKHSs
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}
for which the intersection is a non-trivial subspace. This problem is more challenging and we combine the direct sum approach with a quotient space approach to deal with it. We conclude this section by applying this approach to approximate simultaneously
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
,
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}
, which allows us to calculate the least squares error for RKHS functions using only a core set of the data.

3.4.1
Compressing the covariance and weighted mean embedding simultaneously

One of the main challenges when trying to control the approximation error of
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
simultaneously is to determine the size of the convex set that contains
(\mathfrak{C}_{n},\mathfrak{m}_{y,n})
within the direct sum of two RKHSs and to locate
(\mathfrak{C}_{n},\mathfrak{m}_{y,n})
within this convex set, or, alternatively, to analyze the covariance operator corresponding to this new space. These problems would be easier to handle if we could identify the direct sum space with an RKHS and apply the techniques that we have developed for RKHSs. When using the first approach for
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
, we face directly a problem in that we will gain some weighted sum of
(\kappa(X_{i},\cdot),k(X_{i},\cdot))
as an approximation, but we need a weighted sum of
(\kappa(X_{i},\cdot),Y_{i}k(X_{i},\cdot))
. This problem can be circumvented by incorporating the
Y_{i}
’s into the kernel as we have done in the second approach, i.e. for a given kernel function
k
on
\mathcal{X}
let,

\rho((y_{1},x_{1}),(y_{2},x_{2}))=y_{1}y_{2}k(x_{1},x_{2})=\langle\langle y_{1},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes k(x_{1},\cdot),\langle y_{2},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes k(x_{2},\cdot)\rangle_{\otimes}

(40)

then
\rho
is a kernel function on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{X}
and we move from
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}
to
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}
. It helps to also extend
\kappa
to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{X}
by setting
\kappa_{y}((y_{1},x_{1}),(y_{2},x_{2}))=\kappa(x_{1},x_{2})
. Let
\hat{h}
be the extension of
h\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}
to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{X}
, i.e.
\hat{h}(y,x)=h(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\mathbb{R}
, then

\|\hat{h}\|_{\kappa_{y}}=\|h\|_{\kappa}
. For finite linear combinations this follows from
\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\kappa_{y}((y_{i},x_{i}),\cdot)\|^{2}_{\kappa_{y}}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\kappa_{y}((y_{i},x_{i}),(y_{j},x_{j}))=\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\kappa(x_{i},\cdot)\|^{2}_{\kappa}
, where
n\in\mathbb{N},\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{R},x_{i}\in\mathcal{X},y_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
for all
i\leq n
and extends to all of
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}
by a denseness argument. By a similar argument we can see that the extension map is surjective.

Observe that
\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}
, that is the RKHS corresponding to
\kappa_{y}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}={\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
are linearly independent, i.e.
(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})\cap({\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H})=\{0\}
, because
\kappa_{y}((y_{1},x_{1}),(y_{2},x_{2}))
does not depend on the values
y_{1},y_{2}
while
\rho
does.
Due to this linear independence
we have that
\mathcal{K}=(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})\oplus({\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H})
is isometrically isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa_{y}+\rho}
:
Let
\mathcal{G}=\{g+h:(g,h)\in\mathcal{K}\}
with
norm
\|f\|_{\mathcal{G}}=\inf\{\|(g,h)\|_{\mathcal{K}}:g+h=f,(g,h)\in\mathcal{K}\}
. There exists a surjective isometry between
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{G}
. Because
\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
are linearly independent there is for every
f\in\mathcal{G}
exactly one pair
(g,h)\in\mathcal{K}
such that
g+h=f
and
\|f\|_{\mathcal{G}}=\|(g,h)\|_{\mathcal{K}}
. Furthermore, we have an inner product on
\mathcal{G}
which is given by

\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}=\langle(g_{1},h_{1}),(g_{2},h_{2})\rangle_{\mathcal{K}}
whenever
f_{1}=g_{1}+h_{1}
and
f_{2}=g_{2}+h_{2}
.
For
(g,h)\in\mathcal{K}
we have that
g\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa_{y}}
and
h\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
. By
[
2
, Thm.,p.353]
the kernel
\kappa_{y}+\rho
is the kernel of
\mathcal{G}
and, therefore,
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa_{y}+\rho}
is isometrically isomorphic to
\mathcal{K}
.

When
P
is a Radon measure with support
S\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}
then we can look at
\mathcal{K}_{S}=(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})_{S}\oplus(\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}
, where
(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})_{S}=\{u\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}:u\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}\}=\mathcal{H}_{\kappa_{y}\!\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}
with norm

\|u\|_{\kappa_{y}\!\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}=\inf\{\|v\|:u=v\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f},v\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}\}
, with
S_{f}=\{(y,x):(x,y)\in S\}
, and similarly we define
(\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}
. If
\mathbb{R}_{S}=\{y:(x,y)\in S\}
contains at least two elements then
(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})_{S}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}=\{0\}
and the above argument shows that
\mathcal{K}_{S}
is isometrically isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}_{(\kappa_{y}+\rho)\!\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}
. We summarize this in the following lemma.

Lemma 4
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space and
k
a measurable kernel function on
\mathcal{X}
with corresponding RKHS
\mathcal{H}
then

\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}\oplus({\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H})\cong\mathcal{H}_{\kappa_{y}+\rho}.

Furthermore, if
P
is a Radon measure on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}
with support
S
and
\mathbb{R}_{S}
contains at least two elements then

(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})_{S}\oplus(\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}\cong\mathcal{H}_{(\kappa_{y}+\rho)\!\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}.

In the following, we focus on the case where
P
is a Radon measure and study the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{(\kappa_{y}+\rho)\!\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}
. For ease of notation let
\kappa_{\oplus}=(\kappa_{y}+\rho)\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f}\times S_{f}
. Similar to before, there is a natural definition for the convex set that contains our mean element. This convex set is

C_{\kappa_{\oplus}}=\text{{cch}\,}\{\kappa_{\oplus}((y,x),\cdot):(x,y)\in S\}

and the empirical analogue is

C_{\kappa_{\oplus},n}=\text{{cch}\,}\{\kappa_{\oplus}((Y_{i},X_{i}),\cdot):i\leq n\}.

The mean element that we want to approximate is then
\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa_{\oplus}}=\int\kappa_{\oplus}((y,x),\cdot)\,dP(x,y)
when this is well defined, and the empirical analogue is
\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa_{\oplus},n}=(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{\oplus}((Y_{i},X_{i}),\cdot)
.

In the following, we will assume that
Y=f_{0}(X)+\epsilon
with both
f_{0}
and
\epsilon
being bounded and
\epsilon
independent of
X
.

Covariance operator

We denote the covariance operator corresponding to
\kappa_{\oplus}
by
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{\kappa_{\oplus}}
. Because we are dealing with a direct sum one might suppose that it follows directly that the covariance operator factors into the individual covariance operators corresponding to
\kappa_{y}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f}\times S_{f}
and
\rho\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f}\times S_{f}
. Unfortunately that is not the case: for
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa_{\oplus}}
there exists
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa_{y}\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}
and
g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}
such that
h_{i}=f_{i}+g_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, and
\|h_{i}\|^{2}_{\kappa_{\oplus}}=\|f_{i}\|^{2}_{\kappa_{y}\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}+\|g_{i}\|^{2}_{\rho\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{\kappa_{\oplus}}h_{1},h_{2}\rangle_{\kappa_{\oplus}}=

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{\kappa_{y}\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{\kappa_{y}\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}+\langle\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{\rho\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}g_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{\rho\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}

\displaystyle+E(f_{1}\times g_{2})+E(f_{2}\times g_{1}).

(41)

The cross-terms do not vanish even if we use the centered covariance operator.

Width of
C_{\kappa_{\oplus}}

We can apply our standard approach directly to the kernel function
\kappa_{\oplus}
(recall that in our definition of this kernel the reduction to the support of
P
is already incorporated) to gain insights into the convex set
C_{\kappa_{\oplus}}
. Alternatively, we can aim to link the width of
C_{\kappa_{\oplus}}
back to the width of the corresponding convex sets corresponding to the kernel
k
and
\kappa
.
Due to Lemma
4
we have that

\text{{width}\,}_{u}C_{\oplus}=\text{{width}\,}_{h}C_{\kappa_{\oplus}},

where
u\in(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})_{S}\oplus(\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S},\|u\|=1
,
h
, with
\|h\|=1
, is the corresponding element in
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa_{\oplus}}
, and

C_{\oplus}=\text{{cch}\,}\{((\kappa_{y}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}\times S_{f})((y,x),\cdot),(\rho\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f}\times S_{f})((y,x),\cdot)\}\subset(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})_{S}\oplus(\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}.

Hence, we can bound the width of
C_{\oplus}
instead of bounding directly the width of
C_{\kappa_{\oplus}}
. We can write any
u\in(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})_{S}\oplus(\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}
as
(\hat{g}\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f},v\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!S_{f})
, where
g\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
,
\hat{g}
is the extension of
g
to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{X}
, and
v\in{\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
. Observe that if
v
is given by a finite linear combination of elements
\langle y_{i},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes h_{i}
,
y_{i}\in\mathbb{R},h_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
, then

v=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}(\langle y_{i},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes h_{i})=\langle 1,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}\alpha_{i}h_{i}\bigr).

(42)

For such finite linear combinations let
\psi:{\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
be
\psi(v)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}\alpha_{i}h_{i}
. The map
\psi
is independent of the particular representation of
v
because if

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}(\langle y_{i},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes h_{i})=v=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\beta_{i}(\langle z_{i},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes g_{i})

for a suitable
m\in\mathbb{N}
and corresponding
\beta_{i},z_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
,
g_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
for all
i\leq m
, then

0=\|\langle 1,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes(\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}\alpha_{i}h_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}\beta_{i}g_{i})\|^{2}_{\rho}=\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}\alpha_{i}h_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}\beta_{i}g_{i}\|^{2}

We can also observe that
\|v\|^{2}_{\rho}=\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}\alpha_{i}h_{i}\|^{2}=\|\psi(v)\|^{2}
. Furthermore,
\psi
is linear and therefore an isometry. Since the finite linear combinations lie dense in
{\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}
, and both
{\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}
are complete, we can extend
\psi
to a surjective isometry between
{\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}

[
15
, Cor.4.3.18]
. In particular, any

v\in{\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
can be represented as
\psi^{-1}(h)
with a unique
h\in\mathcal{H}
.

The width of
C_{\oplus}
can now be lower bounded in the following way: choose
\alpha>0
, let
b_{\alpha}=\sup\{b:\Pr(\epsilon\geq b)>\alpha\text{ and }\Pr(\epsilon\leq-b)>\alpha\}
and
I=[-b_{\alpha},b_{\alpha}]
. Then

\displaystyle\text{{width}\,}_{(\hat{g}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f},\psi^{-1}(h)\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f})}C_{\oplus}=\!\!\!\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{S},z\in I}\!\!\!\!(g(x)+(f_{0}(x)+z)h(x))-\!\!\!\infd_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{S},z\in I}\!\!\!\!\!(g(x)+(f_{0}(x)+z)h(x))

whenever
(\hat{g}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f},\psi^{-1}(h)\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f})
has norm one,
g\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
and
h\in\mathcal{H}
. In particular, when choosing the same point
x
and using
z
to move to absolute values, we gain

\text{{width}\,}_{(\hat{g}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f},\psi^{-1}(h)\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f})}C_{\oplus}\geq 2\|h\|b_{\alpha}\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{S}}\frac{|h(x)|}{\|h\|}\geq b_{\alpha}\|h\|\text{{width}\,}_{h/\|h\|}(C_{\mathcal{X}_{S}}),

(43)

where
C_{\mathcal{X}_{S}}
is the usual convex set for the kernel
k\!\!\upharpoonright\!\!\mathcal{X}_{S}\times\mathcal{X}_{S}
.

We need to complement this bound with a bound that is based on
g
to deal with cases where
\|h\|
is small.
When
\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}
is smaller than
b_{\alpha}
then there is a simple way to get a lower bound that involves
g
. For two points
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{S}
and any
h\in\mathcal{H}
, we can chose
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{S}
such that
(f_{0}(x_{1})+z_{1})h(x_{1})\geq 0\geq(f_{0}(x_{2})+z_{2})h(x_{2})
and, hence,

\text{{width}\,}_{(\hat{g}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f},\psi^{-1}(h)\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f})}C_{\oplus}\geq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{S}}g(x)-\infd_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{S}}g(x)\geq\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}\,\text{{width}\,}_{g/\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}}C_{\odot}.

(44)

We can combine (
43
) and (
44
) to gain
a lower bound on the width of
C_{\oplus}
in terms of the widths of
C
and
C_{\odot}
.

The low noise setting.

The situation gets more complicated when
|f_{0}|
attains values that are significantly larger than
b_{\alpha}
. For instance, when there is no noise, i.e.
\epsilon=0
(a.s.), and there exists some
h\in\mathcal{H},g\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
such that
h(x)\not=0
and
f_{0}(x)=-g(x)/h(x)
on
\mathbb{R}_{S}
, and
(\hat{g}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f},\psi^{-1}(h)\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f})
has norm one, it holds that
\text{{width}\,}_{(\hat{g}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f},\psi^{-1}(h)\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f})}C_{\oplus}=0
. For
f_{0}
to be equal or close to
-g/h
it is necessary that
f_{0}
attains large values when
\|h\|
is small. For example, when
\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
is finite dimensional with dimension
d
,
\lambda_{d}>0
is the smallest eigenvalue of a suitable kernel matrix based on the kernel
\kappa
, and
k
is a bounded kernel, then
f_{0}(x)=-g(x)/h(x)
can only happen if

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|f_{0}(x)|\geq\frac{\|g\|_{\infty}}{\|h\|_{\infty}}\geq\frac{\|g\|\lambda_{d}^{1/2}}{d^{1/2}\|h\|\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}}.

For a small value of
\|h\|
this implies that
\|g\|
will be close to
1
and
|f_{0}|
has to attain a large value at some locations
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

Interpolation and another look at the low noise setting

We look now at the case where there is no noise at all, that is
Y=f_{0}(X)
,

C_{\oplus}=\text{{cch}\,}\{(\kappa_{y}((f_{0}(x),x),\cdot),\langle f_{0}(x),\cdot\rangle_{{\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}}\otimes k(x,\cdot)):x\in\mathcal{X}\}

and we are interested in interpolating
f_{0}
.
In particular, we are controlling the width of
C_{\oplus}
depending on how
f_{0}
is related to
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
. The direct sum approach is useful to gain a deeper understanding of how well
(\mathfrak{C}_{y},\mathfrak{m}_{y}^{\otimes})
can be approximated. The width of
C_{\oplus}
in this interpolation setting has a simple form.
Assume that the support of the marginal measure is all of
\mathcal{X}
and since there is no noise it then follows that the support of the measure
P
is
S=\{(x,f_{0}(x)):x\in\mathcal{X}\}
. For
g\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H},h\in\mathcal{H}
,

\text{{width}\,}_{(\hat{g},\psi^{-1}(h))}C_{\oplus}=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}(g(x)+f_{0}(x)h(x))-\infd_{x\in\mathcal{X}}(g(x)+f_{0}(x)h(x))

The functions
f_{0}\times h
lie in the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{f_{0}}
which has the kernel function
k_{0}(x,y)=f_{0}(x)k(x,y)f_{0}(y)
. According to
[
26
, Prop.5.20]
the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{f_{0}}
is equal to
\{f_{0}\times h:h\in\mathcal{H}\}
and the inner product on
\mathcal{H}_{f_{0}}
is given by
\langle f_{0}\times h_{1},f_{0}\times h_{2}\rangle_{f_{0}}=\langle h_{1},h_{2}\rangle
whenever
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
. If
\mathcal{H}_{f_{0}}\cap(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})=\{0\}
then we can embed both
\mathcal{H}_{f_{0}}
and
\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
in
the direct sum
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})\oplus\mathcal{H}_{f_{0}}
such that for any
f\in\mathcal{H}_{f_{0}},h\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
it holds that
\|f\|_{f_{0}}=\|(0,f)\|_{\mathcal{G}}
and
\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}=\|(h,0)\|_{\mathcal{G}}
. As in Lemma
4
it holds that
\mathcal{G}\cong\mathcal{H}_{k_{0}+\kappa}
and, therefore, it also holds that
\|f\|_{k_{0}}=\|f\|_{k_{0}+\kappa}
and
\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}=\|h\|_{k_{0}+\kappa}
. In this case,

\text{{width}\,}_{(\hat{g},\psi^{-1}(h))}=\text{{width}\,}_{(g,f_{0}\times h)}C_{\mathcal{G}}

whenever
g\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H},h\in\mathcal{H}
, where
C_{\mathcal{G}}=\text{{cch}\,}\{(\kappa(x,\cdot),k_{0}(x,\cdot)):x\in\mathcal{X}\}\subset\mathcal{G}
. This follows directly from

\displaystyle g(x)+f_{0}(x)h(x)=\langle(g,f_{0}\times h),(\kappa(x,\cdot),k_{0}(x,\cdot))\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}.

We can now follow the approach from Section
2.3.3
and, in particular, apply Proposition
1
to the RKHS with kernel
k_{f_{0}}+\kappa
. Assumptions on
f_{0}
imply then lower bounds on the width.

3.4.2
Linearly dependent spaces

The setting above where we approximate
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\otimes}_{y,n}
simultaneously is easy to deal with because the corresponding RKHSs are linearly independent.
On the other hand, when the spaces over which we want to optimize are linearly dependent then the RKHS is not isometrically isomorphic to the direct product space and the approach needs to be modified. This can happen, for example, when we try to approximate
\mathfrak{C}
simultaneously to
\mathfrak{m}
. In this context the corresponding spaces
\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}
can overlap. For instance, when
k
is a polynomial kernel of order two then
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
are not linearly independent.

Whenever
\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}
are not linearly independent it is natural to identify elements like
(h,0)
and
(0,h)
,
h\in(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})\cap\mathcal{H}
. One way to do so is to consider the subspace
U=\{(-h,h):h\in(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})\cap\mathcal{H}\}
of
\mathcal{K}:=(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})\oplus\mathcal{H}
. The subspace is closed: let
\{(-h_{n},h_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a convergent sequence in
U
. This sequence is also a Cauchy sequence and for any
\epsilon>0
there exists an
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n,m\geq\mathbb{N}
,

\epsilon>\|(-h_{n},h_{n})-(-h_{m},h_{m})\|_{\oplus}^{2}=\|h_{m}-h_{n}\|^{2}+\|h_{n}-h_{m}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}

and
\{h_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence both in
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
. Hence, it converges in both spaces. Let
f
be its limit in

\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
and
g
its limit in
\mathcal{H}
then for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
there exists an
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that

|f(x)-g(x)|\leq\epsilon+|h_{n}(x)-h_{n}(x)|=\epsilon
and
f=g
. It also follows right away that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|(-h_{n},h_{n})-(-f,g)\|_{\oplus}=0
and the sequence has its limit in
U
.

Consider the quotient space
\mathcal{K}/U
with co-sets
f^{\bullet}=f+U
,
f\in\mathcal{K}
, and the quotient norm
\|f^{\bullet}\|_{\mathcal{K}/U}=\inf\{\|f+h\|_{\mathcal{K}}:h\in U\}
. The space
\mathcal{K}/U
is again a Hilbert space since
U
is closed (e.g.
[
28
, Sec.III.4]
), and it is isometrically isomorphic to the Hilbert space

\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}+\mathcal{H}
when the latter is equipped with the norm
\|f\|_{+}^{2}=\inf\{\|g\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}+\|h\|^{2}:f=g+h,g\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H},h\in\mathcal{H}\}
;
in particular, a co-set
(g,h)+U\in\mathcal{K}/U
is mapped to the function
f=g+h
. This map is well defined since if
(g_{1},h_{1})\in(g,h)^{\bullet}
then there is some
h_{2}
such that
g_{1}+h_{1}=g-h_{2}+h+h_{2}=f
. Furthermore, by the choice of
U
, there are no two elements
u^{\bullet},v^{\bullet}\in\mathcal{K}/U
,
u^{\bullet}\not=v^{\bullet}
, that are mapped to the same function
f
. Assume otherwise, then there is some
f
such that
f=g_{1}+h_{1}=g_{2}+h_{2}
and, therefore,
(g_{2}+g_{1}-g_{2},h_{2}-g_{1}+g_{2})=(g_{1},h_{1})
. Since
g_{1}-g_{2}\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
and
g_{1}-g_{2}=h_{1}-h_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
it
follows that
(g_{2},h_{2})^{\bullet}=(g_{1},h_{1})^{\bullet}
which contradicts the assumption. Finally, any element in
\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}+\mathcal{H}
can be represented this way since if
f=g+h
,
g\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H},h\in\mathcal{H}
then
(g,h)^{\bullet}
is mapped to
f
.
Using again
[
2
, Thm.,p.353]
we can conclude that
\mathcal{K}/U
and
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa+k}
are isometrically isomorphic.

While
\mathcal{K}/U
and
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa+k}
are isometrically isomorphic it does not hold in general that
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}/U
are isometrically isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa+k}
. Hence, when mapping an element
u\in\mathcal{K}
to
u^{\bullet}\in\mathcal{K}/U
, then finding an approximation
v^{\bullet}
of
u^{\bullet}
in
\mathcal{K}/U
, we generally cannot invert the
\bullet
operation to gain an approximation of
u
. Selecting an arbitrary element in
v^{\bullet}
does not work either since a small value of
\|u^{\bullet}-v^{\bullet}\|_{\mathcal{K}/U}
does not imply that all elements in the corresponding co-sets have small distances, i.e. there is no reason why
\sup_{w\in v^{\bullet}}\|u-w\|_{\mathcal{K}}
should be small. However, we are no trying to approximate arbitrary elements in
\mathcal{K}
but only elements

(\mathfrak{C},\mathfrak{m})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\kappa(X_{i},\cdot),k(X_{i},\cdot))

and we are optimizing the approximation over
\tilde{C}=\text{{cch}\,}\{(\kappa(x,\cdot),k(x,\cdot)):x\in\mathcal{X}\}\subset\mathcal{K}
. The important observation is that for any non-zero element
(-h,h)\in U
, that is
h\in(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})\cap\mathcal{H}
, we have

\langle(-h,h),(\kappa(x,\cdot),k(x,\cdot))\rangle_{\mathcal{K}}=-h(x)+h(x)=0,

and
\tilde{C}
is a subset of
U^{\perp}
.

The subspace
U^{\perp}
together with the inner product inherited from
\mathcal{K}
is isometrically isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa+k}
. This follows since
\mathcal{K}/U
and
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa+k}
are isometrically isomorphic and
U^{\perp}
and
\mathcal{K}/U
are isometrically isomorphic. The latter holds since every co-set corresponds to exactly one element in
U^{\perp}
, and for
u\in U^{\perp}
,
\|u^{\bullet}\|_{\mathcal{K}/U}=\inf\{\|u+v\|_{\mathcal{K}}:v\in U\}=\|u\|_{\mathcal{K}}
.

Also,
\overline{\text{{span}}}\,\tilde{C}=U^{\perp}
. We know already that
\overline{\text{{span}}}\,\tilde{C}\subset U^{\perp}
. To show that they are equal let

\mathcal{K}=\text{{span}\,}((\overline{\text{{span}}}\,\tilde{C})\cup U)
. Observe that this space is closed since
\overline{\text{{span}}}\,\tilde{C}
and
U
are, and because they are orthogonal. It is sufficient to show that
(f,0)\in\mathcal{K},(0,g)\in\mathcal{K}
for all
f\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
and
g\in\mathcal{H}
since the smallest closed subspace that contains all these elements is
(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})\oplus\mathcal{H}
.

For
f=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\beta_{i}(\kappa(x_{i},\cdot)+k(x_{i},\cdot))\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa+k}
define
\psi(f)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\beta_{i}(\kappa(x_{i},\cdot),k(x_{i}\cdot))\in\overline{\text{{span}}}\,\tilde{C}\subset(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})\oplus\mathcal{H}
. The operator
\psi:\mathcal{H}_{\kappa+k}\to\overline{\text{{span}}}\,\tilde{C}
is linear and defined on a dense subset of
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa+k}
. It is furthermore norm preserving since

\|\psi(f)\|^{2}_{\oplus}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\beta_{i}\beta_{j}\kappa(x_{i},x_{j})+\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\beta_{i}\beta_{j}k(x_{i},x_{j})=\|f\|_{\kappa+k}^{2}.

Hence, it can be extended to a linear isometry, which we will also denote by
\psi
, between
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa+k}
and
\overline{\text{{span}}}\,\tilde{C}
with the norm inherited from
(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})\oplus\mathcal{H}
.

For any
h\in(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})\cap\mathcal{H}
we can infer that it lies in the RKHS with kernel
\kappa+k
due to

[
2
, Thm.,p.353]
and
\psi(h)
lies in
\overline{\text{{span}}}\,\tilde{C}
. Write
\psi(h)
as
(h_{1},h_{2})
,
h_{1}\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H},h_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
, then for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle h_{1}(x)+h_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\langle(h_{1},h_{2}),(\kappa(x,\cdot),k(x,\cdot))\rangle_{\oplus}=\langle\psi(h),\psi(\kappa(x,\cdot)+k(x,\cdot))\rangle_{\oplus}

\displaystyle=\langle h,\kappa(x,\cdot)+k(x,\cdot)\rangle_{\kappa+k}=h(x).

In other words, for any
h\in(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})\cap\mathcal{H}
we have
h_{1}\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H},h_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
such that
h=h_{1}+h_{2}
and
(h_{1},h_{2})\in\overline{\text{{span}}}\,\tilde{C}
. Since
h,h_{1}\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
it follows that
h_{2}=h-h_{1}\in(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})\cap\mathcal{H}
and
(h_{2},-h_{2})\in U
. Thus,

(h,0)=(h_{1},h_{2})+(h_{2},-h_{2})\in\mathcal{K}
. Similarly, we can observe that
(0,h)\in\mathcal{K}
.

For
f\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
let
\psi(f)=(f_{1},f_{2})
with
f_{1}\in\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
and
f_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
. In other words,
f=f_{1}+f_{2}
and
since
\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}
is a linear space, we know that
f_{2}=f-f_{1}\in(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})\cap\mathcal{H}
. And, as above, we can conclude that
(f,0)
also lies in
\mathcal{K}
. The same argument also shows that for any
g\in\mathcal{H}
we have
(0,g)\in\mathcal{K}
. Hence,
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})\oplus\mathcal{H}
and

\overline{\text{{span}}}\,\tilde{C}=U^{\perp}
.

Lemma 5
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space and
k
a measurable kernel function on
\mathcal{X}
with corresponding RKHS
\mathcal{H}
then
\overline{\text{{span}}}\,\{(\kappa(x,\cdot),k(x,\cdot)):x\in\mathcal{X}\}\subset(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})\oplus\mathcal{H}
equipped with the inner product of
(\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H})\oplus\mathcal{H}

is isometrically isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa+k}
.

3.4.3
Simultaneous least-squares risk approximation for unbounded
Y

Often it is unnecessary to include the
(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}^{2}
term in the simultaneous approximation since many methods only rely on the terms that include
f
(e.g. the ridge regressor) and also
(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}
itself can be represented by a single real number and does not need to be compressed. However, when selecting points
(X_{\iota(1)},Y_{\iota(m)})
,
m\ll n
, for a coreset then

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(f^{2}(X_{\iota(i)})-2Y_{\iota(i)}f(X_{\iota(i)}))+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}^{2}

is not the mean squared error of
f
given the sample
X_{\iota(1)},Y_{\iota(1)},\ldots,X_{\iota(m)},Y_{\iota(m)}
and might even be negative.
An easy way to remedy this problem is to move to
(1/m)\sum_{i=1}^{m}Y_{\iota(i)}^{2}
but then we do not have any guarantee that this is close
to
(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}^{2}
. An alternative is to include the
Y_{i}
’s in the simultaneous approximation problem. This can be done by, for instance,
defining a kernel on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{X}
through
r((y_{1},x_{1}),(y_{2},x_{2}))=\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}
and by considering the direct sum

\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}\oplus({\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H})\oplus\mathcal{H}_{r}.

Alternatively, we can restrict the functions to the support
S
of the underlying measure and consider

(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})_{S}\oplus({\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{r\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}.

If there is
no constant function in the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{X}_{S}}
then
({\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}\cap\mathcal{H}_{r\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}=\{0\}
and

(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})_{S}\cap(({\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}\cap\mathcal{H}_{r\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}})=\{0\}

if, furthermore,
\mathbb{R}_{S}
contains at least two different values
: any function in
\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}}
is of the form
g^{2}(x),x\in\mathcal{X}_{S},g\in\mathcal{H}
and functions in

({\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}\cap\mathcal{H}_{r\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}})
are of the form
(y,x)\mapsto yh(x)+cy
for some constant
c
. For any functions
g,h\in\mathcal{H}
, choose
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{S}
,
y_{1}\not=y_{2}
, and
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{S}
are such that
h(x_{1})\not=h(x_{2})
. For
g^{2}
to be equal to
yh(x)+cy
it has to hold that
g^{2}(x_{1})
is equal to
y_{1}h(x_{1})+cy_{1}
and it also has to be equal to
y_{2}h(x_{1})+cy_{2}
In other words,

(y_{1}-y_{2})h(x_{1})=c(y_{2}-y_{1})
and
h(x_{1})=-c
and similarly for
h(x_{2})
, that is
h(x_{1})=h(x_{2})
with a contraction to the choice of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. Hence, under these conditions we can identify the direct sum with an RKHS corresponding to a sum of kernels,

(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})_{S}\oplus({\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{r\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}\cong\mathcal{H}_{(\kappa_{y}+\rho+r)\!\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}.

When the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{X}_{S}}
contains the constant function then the intersection
(\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}\cap\mathcal{H}_{r\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}
is not empty since the function
(y,x)\mapsto y
, with domain
S
, lies in
(\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}
and in
\mathcal{H}_{r\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}
. We can follow the same approach as in Section
3.4.2
and consider the one dimensional subspace
U=\{(-h,h):h\in(\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}\cap\mathcal{H}_{r\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}\}
of
({\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{r\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}
and consider the quotient space

\mathcal{Q}=(({\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{r\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}})/U
with the usual quotient norm. The space
\mathcal{Q}
is a Hilbert space
[
28
, Sec.III.4]
. By the same argument as in Section
3.4.2
we can infer that

\mathcal{Q}\cong\mathcal{H}_{(\rho+r)\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}

and for
(x,y)\in S
,
(\rho((y,x),\cdot),r((y,x),\cdot))
lies in
U^{\perp}\subseteq({\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{r\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}
. Also, the space
U^{\perp}
, with the inherited inner product, is isometric isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}_{(\rho+r)\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}
.

When
\mathbb{R}_{S}
contains at least two elements
then
(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})_{S}\cap(({\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}\cap\mathcal{H}_{r\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}})=\{0\}
and

(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})_{S}\oplus\mathcal{Q}\cong\mathcal{H}_{(\kappa_{y}+\rho+r)\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}.

We will apply these results to the problem of ridge regression and it is convenient to have a proposition which provides guarantees on the approximation in the ridge regression context. Since we do not need to approximate the sum of the
Y_{i}^{1}
terms to compute the ridge regression estimator we will consider the space
(\widehat{\mathcal{H}\odot\mathcal{H}})_{S}\cap({\mathbb{R}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{S}
. We make the assumption that
\mathcal{H}
does not contain the constant functions
, which removes the need to consider quotient spaces. Furthermore, we will assume
sub-Gaussian noise
and that
f_{0}
is bounded
but we will allow for
unbounded
Y_{i}
random variables
. Recall the definitions of the kernel functions
\rho
in (
40
),
\kappa:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\kappa=k^{2}
, and its extension
\kappa_{y}
(see just below (
40
)). Before stating a result on the compression, we need to modify the arguments that we used to control the difference between
\mathfrak{m}_{y,n}^{\otimes}
and
\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}_{y,n}^{\otimes}
, and the difference between
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{\otimes,S}_{c}
and
\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{\otimes,S_{n}}_{c}
. This is necessary since the kernel function, which we will denote by
\tau
below, is
(\kappa_{y}+\rho)((y,x),(y^{\prime},x^{\prime}))=\kappa(x,x^{\prime})+yy^{\prime}k(x,x^{\prime})
in the current context, and this kernel function is not of the form
yy^{\prime}\tilde{k}(x,x^{\prime})
, where
\tilde{k}
is some kernel on
\mathcal{X}
. Since we assumed that latter form in Section
3.3.3
when we derived the bounds on the differences, we cannot simply reuse the earlier results. Fortunately, the necessary modifications are minor: Let
\mathfrak{m}_{\tau,n}
be the empirical mean element corresponding to the kernel function
\tau
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{X}

and

\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}_{\tau,n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\leq n}\tau((\wideparen{Y}_{i}^{(n)},X_{i}),\cdot)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\leq n}(\kappa(X_{i},\cdot)+\langle\wideparen{Y}_{i}^{(n)},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}k(X_{i},\cdot)).

The distance between the empirical mean element and its capped version is

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{m}_{\tau,n}-\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}_{\tau,n}\|^{2}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\|\sum_{i\leq n}\langle Y_{i}-\wideparen{Y}_{i}^{(n)},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}k(X_{i},\cdot)\|^{2}_{\tau}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}\|\sum_{i\leq n}\langle Y_{i}-\wideparen{Y}_{i}^{(n)},\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}k(X_{i},\cdot)\|^{2}_{\rho}

since the
\kappa
terms cancel and because the function inside the norm lies within
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
(apply
[
26
, Thm.5.4]
to get the inequality).
This implies that we can reuse the bound in (
35
), and with probability
1-\delta
,
\delta\in(0,1)
, we have that

\|\mathfrak{m}_{\tau,n}-\wideparen{\mathfrak{m}}_{\tau,n}\|^{2}_{\tau}\leq\sqrt{2\nu}\|k\|^{1/2}_{\infty}e^{-r_{n}^{2}/4\nu}\Bigl(2/n+\pi e^{-r_{n}^{2}/2\nu}\Bigr)^{1/2}\delta^{-1/2},

(45)

where
\nu>0
is the variance factor corresponding to the sub-Gaussian noise terms.
We also need control over the covariance operators corresponding to the capped
Y_{i}
’s. We proceed as in Section
3.3.3
. Assuming that the law
P
of
(Y,X)
is a Radon measure let
S
be its support and let
S_{n}
be the support of
(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X)
(which is well defined as the law of this random variable is again a Radon measure). Let
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c,\tau}^{S}:\mathcal{H}_{\tau}\to\mathcal{H}_{\tau}
be the covariance operator corresponding to the original random variable and
\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{S_{n}}_{c,\tau}:\mathcal{H}_{\tau}\to\mathcal{H}_{\tau}
the covariance operator corresponding to the capped random variable. We start by bounding the difference between these covariance operators in the operator norm,

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c,\tau}^{S}-\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{S_{n}}_{c,\tau}\|_{op}=\sup_{\|h_{1}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f}\|_{\tau,S_{f}}=1}\sup_{\|h_{2}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f}\|_{\tau,S_{f}}=1}\langle(\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c,\tau}^{S}-\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{S_{n}}_{c,\tau})h_{1}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f},h_{2}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f}\rangle_{\tau,S_{f}}.

Due to Lemma
4
the space
\mathcal{H}_{\tau,S_{f}}
is isometrically isomorphic to a direct sum space and, as above (
41
), we can write
h_{i}=f_{i}+g_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa_{y}\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}},g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho\upharpoonright S_{f}\times S_{f}}
and such that the squared norms of the
h_{i}
equals the sum of the squared norms of the
f_{i}
and
g_{i}
. We can proceed by expanding the
h_{i}
’s and observing that
f_{i}(\wideparen{y},x)=f_{i}(y,x)
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
since
f_{i}
is a function of the second coordinate only,

\displaystyle\langle(\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c,\tau}^{S}-\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{S_{n}}_{c,\tau})h_{1}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f},h_{2}\!\upharpoonright\!S_{f}\rangle_{\tau,S_{f}}

\displaystyle=E(h_{1}(Y,X)h_{2}(Y,X))-E(h_{1}(Y,X))E(h_{2}(Y,X))

\displaystyle\quad-E(h_{1}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X)h_{2}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))+E(h_{1}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))E(h_{2}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X)).

The difference of the bias terms becomes

\displaystyle E(h_{1}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))E(h_{2}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))-E(h_{1}(Y,X))E(h_{2}(Y,X))

\displaystyle=E(g_{1}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))E(g_{2}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))-E(g_{1}(Y,X))E(g_{2}(Y,X))

\displaystyle\quad+E(f_{1}(Y,X))(E(g_{2}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))-E(g_{2}(Y,X)))

\displaystyle\quad+E(f_{2}(Y,X))(E(g_{1}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))-E(g_{1}(Y,X))).

Since
f_{1},f_{2}
have norm one it follows that
E(f_{1}(Y,X))
and
E(f_{2}(Y,X))
are upper bounded by
\|\kappa\|_{\infty}^{1/2}=\|k\|_{\infty}
. Also recall that
g_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, can be written as
\check{u}_{i}
for some
u_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
and
u_{i}
has norm one (see (
32
)). Hence

|E(g_{i}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))-E(g_{i}(Y,X)))|\leq\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}E(|\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}-Y|)

and the bound (
33
) can be used. Similarly,

\displaystyle|E(g_{1}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))E(g_{2}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))-E(g_{1}(Y,X))E(g_{2}(Y,X))|

\displaystyle\leq|E(g_{1}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))|\,|E(g_{2}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))-E(g_{2}(Y,X))|

\displaystyle\quad+|E(g_{2}(Y,X))|\,|E(g_{1}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))-E(g_{1}(Y,X))|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|k\|_{\infty}E(|\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}-Y|)(E(|Y-f_{0}(X)|)+E(|f_{0}(X)|))

\displaystyle\leq 2\|k\|_{\infty}(\sigma+\|f_{0}\|_{\infty})E(|\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}-Y|),

where we assume that the noise term has variance
\sigma^{2}>0
and
f_{0}
is bounded and measurable. Hence,

\displaystyle|E(h_{1}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))E(h_{2}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))-E(h_{1}(Y,X))E(h_{2}(Y,X))|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|k\|_{\infty}(\sigma+\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2})E(|\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}-Y|).

We can deal with the covariance terms in the same way,

\displaystyle E(h_{1}(Y,X)h_{2}(Y,X))-E(h_{1}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X)h_{2}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))

\displaystyle=E(g_{1}(Y,X)g_{2}(Y,X))-E(g_{1}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X)g_{2}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X))

\displaystyle\quad+E(f_{1}(Y,X)(g_{2}(Y,X)-g_{2}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X)))

\displaystyle\quad+E(f_{2}(Y,X)(g_{1}(Y,X)-g_{1}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X)))

\displaystyle\leq\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2}(E(|Y||g_{1}(Y,X)-g_{1}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X)|)+E(|Y||g_{2}(Y,X)-g_{2}(\wideparen{Y}^{(n)},X)|))

\displaystyle\quad+2\|k\|^{3/2}_{\infty}E(|Y-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}|)

\displaystyle\leq 2\|k\|_{\infty}E(|Y||Y-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}|))+2\|k\|^{3/2}_{\infty}E(|Y-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}|)

\displaystyle\leq 2\|k\|_{\infty}(E(|Y-f_{0}|^{2})^{1/2}E((Y-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)})^{2})^{1/2}+\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}E(|Y-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}|))

\displaystyle\quad+2\|k\|^{3/2}_{\infty}E(|Y-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}|)

\displaystyle=2\sigma\|k\|_{\infty}E((Y-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)})^{2})^{1/2}+2\|k\|_{\infty}(\|f_{0}\|_{\infty}+\|k\|_{\infty}^{1/2})E(|Y-\wideparen{Y}^{(n)}|)

and we can apply (
33
) and (
34
). Combining the above bounds and substituting (
33
) and (
34
) yields the following bound,

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{c,\tau}^{S}-\wideparen{\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}}{\vphantom{\mathfrak{C}}}^{S_{n}}_{c,\tau}\|_{op}\leq
